Abstract: Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) systems are typically evaluated on questions whose surface tokens lexically expose the gold KG path. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. We identify this collapse of path-question alignment as a blind spot in existing KGQA evaluation. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.
  We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic. (iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.

</details>

Abstract: Content moderation is inherently policy-dependent: moderation boundaries are often subtle, subjective, and frequently updated, while conventional rule based or fine-tuning based systems require costly rule rewriting, model retraining, or system redeployment. We propose CaseLaw, a policy-decoupled and training-free framework for adaptive content moderation. CaseLaw reformulates moderation as precedent-based retrieval and voting, where multimodal content is transformed into evidence-preserved structured semantics, embedded into a stable semantic space, and classified by thresholded voting over retrieved precedent labels. We train NoteCap on a large-scale corpus of structurally annotated notes to ensure consistent semantic extraction and freeze it during subsequent policy adaptation, which requires no additional training. Policy adaptation is achieved by updating the semantic case library, including case labels and mappings, without retraining models or modifying prompts. To evaluate CaseLaw, we construct MFO-RedNotes, a benchmark for the male-female opposition risk domain. We additionally provide a five category CoPro comparison. Experiments show that CaseLaw consistently outperforms competing baselines in both precision and recall across policy-dependent risk levels and broader safety categories, with category-wise precision/recall reaching up to 86.8%/90.1% on MFO-RedNotes across both policy settings and 94.5%/92.3% on CoPro. Our work highlights the potential of precedent-based reasoning for policy adaptive content moderation and offers a practical direction toward more stable, transparent, and training-free moderation systems.

</details>

Abstract: As language models (LMs) are increasingly deployed in downstream applications, accurately estimating their likelihood of success on a given example is critical. However, confidence estimates from LMs are poor predictors of their likelihood of success. Accurate confidence estimation is linked in part to the detection of aleatoric uncertainty; we hypothesize that LMs possess representations of at least some aspects of this uncertainty in the form of representations of the inherent ambiguity of the input. Using difference-in-means on minimal pairs of ambiguous and unambiguous sentences, we derive ambiguity representations in LM activations, and find that steering with them selectively (albeit weakly) modulates LMs' judgments of the input's ambiguity. We then propose DART, a fine-tuning method that encourages updates to lie within the span of the ambiguity directions, and penalizes changes to the LM activations outside of this subspace. DART greatly improves calibration, encodes these improvements into the model parameters, and maintains model accuracy on multiple-choice and generative QA tasks.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models are widely used in e-commerce user simulation, providing a foundation for understanding users and predicting human decisions. However, existing simulators still struggle to achieve both personalization and behavioral realism. Most methods predict the next action directly from interaction histories and the current context, without accounting for the evolving mental world that underlies user behavior. Theory of Mind suggests that behavior is driven by mental states, including beliefs, uncertainties, and intentions. Users may interpret the same information in distinct ways, leading to divergent behaviors, while an individual's mental state evolves through interaction and shapes subsequent actions. Ignoring this process prevents simulators from faithfully capturing individual differences and realistic behavior patterns, and undermines its effectiveness in downstream applications. To address this, we propose ShopMind, a Mental World Model for e-commerce user simulation that captures how consumers interpret shopping information and how their mental states evolve, better reflecting individual users and real-world behavior patterns. We further use behavior-mental state pairs synthesized by ShopMind to train a Mental State Reconstruction (MSR) model, which infers consumers' latent mental states from observable behavior. Experiments show that ShopMind improves personalization by 18.0% and behavioral realism by 19.6% over the strongest alternatives. Moreover, MSR improves performance on consumption value measurement and CTR prediction, showing the utility of ShopMind for understanding and predicting real user behavior.

</details>

Abstract: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) produce explicit chains of thought to improve transparency, but they frequently overthink, generating long yet inefficient reasoning chains that inflate computational costs and risk introducing errors. While numerous reinforcement learning methods struggle to penalize verbosity to achieve conciseness, they typically apply the penalty uniformly across lengthy sequences, which leads to credit confounding, that is, the failure to distinguish essential reasoning steps from superfluous ones. In this paper, we study a measurable proxy for this confounding: high-entropy tokens that often coincide with surface transition points where reasoning re-enters verification. Based on this insight, we propose Adaptive Entropy-Sparing (AES), which selectively spares high-entropy tokens that occur before an efficient in-group reference, while penalizing overlength high-entropy suffix tokens associated with superfluous branching. Our AES thereby navigates the suppression-exploration trade-off, reducing overthinking while preserving average reasoning accuracy. We also present an entropy-length model that motivates why local uncertainty can serve as a proxy for future reasoning cost. Across eight reasoning benchmarks, AES reduces reasoning length by up to 52.9% while increasing mean accuracy by up to 3.8% over the base models, consistently outperforming existing efficiency-oriented methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/MobiusDai/AES.

</details>

Existing methods allocate the budget by each domain's learning progress, but offer no guarantee for lagging domains. We therefore introduce distributionally robust optimization (DRO) into MOPD to prioritize the worst-performing domain. However, the per-domain training signals are estimated from a few rollouts and are inherently noisy, so directly applying DRO mistakes noise for the worst case, lets occasional spikes hijack the budget, and protects inherently hard domains rather than truly regressing ones. To address these issues, we propose RDS-MOPD, which keeps the DRO objective and redesigns its three parts: it shifts the allocation only when differences clearly exceed the noise, bounds the influence of any single anomaly, and defines the worst case as the regression from each domain's own best level, all with theoretical guarantees. Experiments show that RDS-MOPD outperforms recent competing methods and ranks first on most mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on reusable natural-language skills, yet growing skill collections remain accumulations of isolated text artifacts that are difficult to compare, deduplicate, and compose. We argue that the reusable content of a skill lies not in its surface wording but in its procedural transitions: which operation to execute under a given state and which observable outcome to produce. Based on this observation, we propose State-Move-Outcome (SMO), a compilation-like framework that decomposes raw skills into reusable procedural bricks and organizes admitted bricks as an externalized symbolic structure. When compiling a skill, SMO first retrieves and composes existing bricks, dynamically connects them through Outcome-to-State conditions, and creates a candidate brick only for an uncovered procedure; the candidate becomes reusable only after validation and admission. Experiments on SkillsBench v1.1 demonstrate that SMO represents 232 rawSkills from 87 underlying tasks with 95 shared bricks, reducing the corresponding skill representations from 366,472 to 149,154 tokens--a $\mathbf{59.3}\times$ reduction and $\mathbf{2.46}\times$ compression. Across 232 skill-conditioned evaluation instances, SMO raises the average task score from $\mathbf{0.6379}$ to $\mathbf{0.7478}$ and increases 5/5 Pass instances from $\mathbf{138}$ to $\mathbf{164}$, while matching or outperforming rawSkill on $\mathbf{208}$ of $\mathbf{232}$ instances. It also reduces average runtime token consumption by $\mathbf{38.38\%}$. These results show that compiling natural-language skills into shared procedural units can jointly improve skill reuse, representation efficiency, and downstream execution.

Rerunning the capped items with a 128K budget halves the accuracy gap but does not close it (93.6% vs. 86.6%): bf16 runs that never finish are twice as common, and 85% of them are exact repetition loops. On Nemotron-Nano-9B-v2, by contrast, 30 of 31 extra caps finish at 128K, so the cost there is mostly extra tokens (exploratory). Same-width bf16 SR removes the length increase in four of five affected models, restores Qwen3.5-9B to 87.6% and brings code length and pass@1 near FP32 levels, so the failure is mostly rounding bias, not missing resolution. With an integer-hash random generator, SR on Qwen3.5-9B matches FP32 on cap hits and accuracy (113 vs. 118; 85.6% for both) at the memory and decoding speed of bf16 RTN. We recommend that evaluations of hybrid models report the state dtype, cap rate and output length alongside accuracy.

Abstract: Scientific discovery requires ideas that are original, useful, and non-obvious. Yet AI scientists are typically given as much task information as possible during idea generation, which can anchor generation toward familiar approaches. Inspired by the cognitive framework of Blind Variation and Selective Retention (BVSR), we propose a discovery framework that separates these two roles of information: it generates proposals under deliberately limited task information to broaden exploration, then learns from proposals previously verified on the same task and their outcomes to retain promising candidates for costly empirical evaluation. We evaluate BVSR across five proposer models and 12 scientific tasks. Limiting task information substantially increases proposal originality and non-obviousness, and produces a larger share of highly creative and high-utility proposals than existing state-of-the-art prompting-based baselines. Learning from prior proposal outcomes on the task further improves candidate selection, discarding half of the candidates while retaining most top-performing proposals. When incorporated into existing AI-scientist workflows, BVSR achieves stronger discoveries across gene expression analysis, drug response prediction, anomaly detection, scaling law discovery, and brain activity prediction. These results suggest that effective AI scientists should use information differently across stages of discovery: constrain task-specific information during exploration while exploiting accumulated empirical experience on the task.

</details>

Abstract: Verbal memory retrieval carries rich diagnostic information for Alzheimer's disease through the order, hesitation, and categorical organization of recalled words, yet computational modeling of this retrieval process remains largely unexplored. A key challenge is that timing and hierarchical structure are diagnostically entangled: the diagnostic meaning of retrieval timing depends on its hierarchical context. We propose TrajLift, a spectral framework that models verbal recall as timed trajectories on a hierarchical graph. Our central mechanism is latency-modulated heat diffusion on the semantic hierarchy, a unified operator that lifts each recalled word into a representation of both multi-scale hierarchical structure and continuous retrieval timing. Formal analysis shows that the resulting representation is first-order separable into structural and temporal components with spectral selectivity across hierarchical levels. On synthetic benchmarks requiring structure-time interaction, TrajLift stays near-perfect on deep hierarchies where all baselines fail. On real-world clinical data, we show that TrajLift outperforms diverse baselines by jointly modeling timing and semantic structure, with gains that grow as retrieval timing becomes more informative.

</details>

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts language models are cheap to run per token but expensive to hold in GPU memory, which drives the cost of serving them in production. One-shot expert compression shrinks them after a single calibration pass. Its two current state-of-the-art methods are REAP for expert pruning, which deletes the least salient experts, and REAM for expert merging, which merges them into the most salient ones. Both have been evaluated only on academic benchmarks. We compare the two on five multilingual production tasks of a web-hosting company, from classification to the generation of marketing HTML email templates, with two 30B-parameter models (Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507 and the hybrid Mamba-attention Nemotron-3.5-Lightning) at 25% and 50% of experts removed and the same calibration data. Pruning wins: on the same rows, REAP is significantly better in 16 of 20 comparisons and REAM in 2. At 25%, REAP keeps 93.6% of Qwen3-30B's generative quality and REAM 62.5%, although on benchmarks the two are within a point for the same model. The damage concentrates in the output contract rather than the content: 89.8-95.2% of each significant loss on email templates comes from hard-gate failures such as invalid JSON, broken mobile layouts and runaway generation, and most failed non-English domain descriptions copy the prompt's English opening phrase. Cross-perplexity and logit divergence, two diagnostics from the REAP paper, also track production quality, as does the rate of repetition loops; n-gram diversity does not. We close with practical guidance for compressing MoE models for production.

Abstract: This technical report presents MNN-Pocket-0.3B, a compact 300M-parameter dense language model designed for efficient on-device deployment and supporting context lengths of up to 32K tokens. With an INT4 deployment footprint of only 173 MB and decoding throughput of up to 466 tokens/s on iPad Pro M5 (Metal), it can be seamlessly integrated into applications on edge devices such as smartphones and smart glasses. MNN-Pocket-0.3B is developed through the co-design of its architecture, data, training pipeline, and inference system. Hardware-aware architecture selection and dependency-aware vocabulary pruning balance model capability with on-device memory and compute constraints. A four-stage pre-training pipeline covering approximately 4.1 trillion high-quality Chinese and English tokens equips the model with broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities. This is followed by a three-stage post-training pipeline comprising instruction fine-tuning, multi-teacher on-policy distillation, and quantization-aware training. Finally, hardware-specific optimizations in the MNN inference engine enable fast, memory-efficient inference and long-context processing. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that MNN-Pocket-0.3B achieves frontier-level performance among models of comparable size, with particularly strong results in Chinese and English knowledge and reasoning, mathematics, coding, and instruction following. It consistently outperforms similarly sized open-source models and even surpasses several 0.5B-parameter models on instruction-following benchmarks. We release MNN-Pocket-0.3B with open weights and ready-to-use MNN deployment packages. We hope it will provide a practical foundation for capable, responsive, and memory-efficient edge applications while advancing research and development in on-device language models.

</details>

Abstract: In addition to the effects of explicit input prompts and instructions, large language model outputs are influenced by underlying behavioral preferences similar to human personality tendencies. These tendencies can affect how models interpret situations and make decisions. However, their influence is difficult to disentangle from the effects of model capabilities and instructions. Therefore, we introduce SPACE, a benchmark framework combining a controlled evaluation protocol with complementary datasets for personality verification, capability assessment, and hierarchical situational tasks. Controlled comparisons across guidance conditions help distinguish personality influence from measured capability changes and immediate instruction effects. Experimental results demonstrate that although prompting can shift generation style, models' internal personality tendencies are persistent and not readily overridden by opposing instructions. Furthermore, these underlying tendencies have an observable influence on LLM task performance, highlighting the need for greater attention to these characteristics and potential applications. Training code and SPACE benchmark specifications are available at https://github.com/Niklauseik/SPACE-training.

Abstract: Despite growing attention to social bias in large language models (LLMs), existing bias benchmarks provide limited coverage of culturally grounded evaluation in Bengali, particularly within Bangladesh's sociocultural context. To address this gap, we develop a bilingual extension of BanglaBBQ comprising 61,312 question-answering instances across 12 social categories in English and Bengali. The benchmark incorporates balanced answer permutations, contrasting question framings, and both ambiguous and disambiguated contexts to systematically examine model performance and stereotype-aligned bias across languages. We validate the dataset through structural checks, 220 statistical tests, and expert annotation of 150 instances by four annotators, achieving strong inter-annotator agreement (Fleiss' \k{appa}=0.8349). We evaluate eight LLMs across 490,496 instances and identify five key findings. First, disambiguated contexts yield substantially greater accuracy improvements in Bengali (+36.59 percentage points) than in English (+17.71). Second, all evaluated models exhibit lower accuracy in Bengali, although Mistral-Nemo shows the smallest cross-lingual accuracy gap (5.12 percentage points). Third, stereotype-aligned bias exhibits language and model dependent variation, increasing in Bengali for Gemma 2 9B but decreasing for Mistral-Nemo. Fourth, higher accuracy does not necessarily correspond to lower stereotype-aligned bias. Finally, bias varies considerably across social categories, with Qwen 2.5 7B exhibiting stronger stereotype alignment in Bengali for Politics and Physical Appearance. These findings demonstrate that cross-lingual performance and social bias are distinct dimensions of LLM behavior and underscore the importance of culturally grounded, language-sensitive benchmarks for evaluating fairness beyond English-centric settings.

</details>

Abstract: Hierarchical multi-label text classification (HMTC) requires models to capture both structural dependencies among labels and the fine-grained semantics of labels at different hierarchy levels. Existing methods often model hierarchy through prediction constraints or representations of individual labels and local relations. Structural and semantic supervision, however, are usually formulated separately. We instead use complete root-to-node label paths as a shared unit for both. Our framework introduces two auxiliary training objectives while retaining a standard multi-label classifier at inference. PathSimNCE aligns document representations with hierarchical paths using hierarchy-aware contrastive targets. These targets provide graded supervision for structurally related paths while preserving explicit supervision for the ground-truth paths. The complementary semantic objective uses LLM-generated, document-conditioned descriptions of the same positive paths. It thereby provides context-specific label semantics beyond static label representations. Experiments on three benchmarks show consistent improvements: structural alignment is particularly beneficial in deeper hierarchies, while semantic alignment provides additional gains for fine-grained labels.

</details>

Abstract: Scientific research has long been driven by substantial human effort, from reviewing prior work and identifying research gaps to designing specific solutions. Recent advances in harness-based agent systems have given rise to increasingly capable Auto Researchers, which can autonomously carry out these tasks and, increasingly, entire end-to-end research workflows. Yet despite their rapidly growing capabilities, how closely the research decisions and outputs produced by auto researchers align with those of human experts remains underexplored. We investigate this gap through the lens of research reconstruction and study: Given comparable research context, how similar are the research decisions and outputs produced by Auto Researchers to those of human researchers in AI research? To answer this question, we introduce AutoResearcher-Human Research Similarity (AutoHRS), a stage-wise evaluation framework for assessing how faithfully auto researchers reconstruct human research artifacts across different stages of the scientific workflow. Using 152 ICLR oral papers as our corpus, we generate 456 research trajectories spanning different stages of the research process. Applying AutoHRS to these trajectories, we find that current Auto Researchers frequently fail to recover key research decisions and their resulting artifacts across multiple stages of the research process.

Abstract: Speculative decoders raise throughput by banking more target tokens per forward pass, and Medusa, EAGLE-3, DFlash and DSpark all do it the same way: more candidates that survive verification. All of them draft in the target's own vocabulary, so one node of the candidate tree is one target token, and the tokenizer makes that unit look like a constant. It is not. BPE's pretokenizer splits on whitespace before merges are learned, so no multi-word string can ever become a single token: in Qwen3-4B, "United States of America" is four tokens and no adjacent pair of them exists as one entry, so a phrase is out of reach of any method drafting in the target's vocabulary.
  We change the unit. A small MLP head reads the frozen target's hidden state and predicts a one- to three-word phrase as one entry in a separate draft vocabulary; greedy longest-match expansion maps it back into the target's own tokens, and N candidates are verified in one forward pass behind a prefix-tree mask, so the change is orthogonal to tree depth and width. The target and its tokenizer stay frozen and a candidate is accepted only while it matches the target's own argmax, so the served output is identical to greedy decoding up to floating-point tie-breaking.
  On one A5000 at batch 1, against HuggingFace generate(), a 21.94M head (0.545% of Qwen3-4B) reaches 2.391x on GSM8K, 2.444x on MATH-500 and 1.544x on MT-Bench. Because the head's output layer is one row of 257 parameters per entry, the draft vocabulary size is the parameter budget: an 8,000-entry head is 3.434M parameters, 0.0854% of the target and 64x smaller than EAGLE-3's drafter, and keeps 92.3% of the larger head's GSM8K tokens per pass. With EAGLE-3 run on same prompts and baseline, our 21.94M head is ahead of tree-16 on both maths sets and behind tree-60 on every set.

Abstract: Multilingual generation requires large language models (LLMs) to determine both what to say and in what language to say it, yet these two commitments are difficult to disentangle from model outputs alone. We use translation as a controlled probe: for multi-parallel segments that a model translates well in one language direction but poorly in another, content is held fixed while language varies. Across three LLMs of different scales and architectures, we find a consistent three-stage pattern of multilingual generation. Intermediate layers first express a default English signal; content information becomes causally available one layer later; and the final target language is resolved only at later layers. The ordering of the English default before content also appears in a reading-comprehension task. Our sparse autoencoder analyses reveal a further dissociation: the intermediate language signal is only weakly represented by sparse features, whereas a later language distinction is clearly sparse, but resists intervention at the patched position. These results provide a mechanistic account of how language selection and content information emerge and interact during multilingual generation.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards ordinarily spreads a single advantage over a whole trajectory, a granularity too coarse to isolate the few decisions on which success in a long, multi-turn interaction actually turns. Privileged self-distillation supplies denser supervision, but it leaves unanswered how a set of local teacher-student discrepancies should be converted into credit along a sequence. We introduce AgentOPSD, a critic-free scheme that assigns credit recursively, one interaction turn at a time. Inside a turn, the token-level log-probability gaps between a skill-conditioned self-teacher and the student are summed, and that sum acts as evidence in a recursive Bayesian update carried out in log-odds space; the resulting revisions reallocate the outcome-derived advantage across turns through a multiplier that is bounded and preserves its sign. With Qwen2.5-3B and Qwen2.5-7B as policy backbones on ALFWorld, the method improves consistently on GRPO and on self-distillation baselines, and attains 89.1% success at the 7B scale. Ablations attribute the gain to aggregating evidence at turn boundaries and to making the belief revision depend on history, rather than to the local gap by itself.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to translate technical explainable AI (XAI) artefacts such as SHAP into explanatory narratives (XAINs) for lay users. In high-risk domains, these explanations are often the main resource through which individuals can understand and contest adverse decisions. Yet LLMs are known to reproduce social bias, even from proxy variables alone, and fairness in XAINs remains unstudied. Because XAINs are bound to a ground-truth artefact, fairness concerns not only what a narrative says and how, but also whether it is equally faithful across social groups. We introduce fairness in XAINs as a new field of research and propose a framework that generates narratives under three conditions (protected attribute disclosed, withheld, or counterfactually swapped), allowing five comparisons that pinpoint the cause of disparities. We apply it to adversely classified instances from four datasets in credit scoring, education and employment, generating narratives with five LLMs, and evaluate faithfulness, feature coverage and sentiment. We find significant disparities across all datasets, metric families and models, arising in two ways. Faithfulness disparities result from feature values: LLMs make plausible but false statements, triggered by values that are unequally distributed across social groups. Feature coverage and sentiment disparities stem from the protected attribute itself: once it is disclosed, LLMs give less explanation to, or frame more negatively the narratives of, groups that have historically been discriminated against or are stereotypically associated with worse outcomes. A more faithful model removes the faithfulness disparities, and withholding protected attributes mostly removes the others, but neither suffices on its own. Fairness in XAINs should therefore be audited separately from the predictive model and its XAI artefact.

</details>

Abstract: The traveling thief problem (TTP) asks a thief to choose a tour and a packing together, and the benchmark of 9,720 instances of Polyakovskiy et al. (2014) is the standard test set for comparing TTP heuristics. We show that four of these instances are not TTP instances at all. Their files give a renting rate of 0.00, so the tour has no effect on the objective and each instance reduces to a 0-1 knapsack problem. One of them belongs to the 60 categorized instances on which new methods are usually compared, and on it five of nine published methods report exactly the same value. The cause is the format of the files, which keep the renting rate with two decimals, while the rule of the generator gives a rate close to 0.003 on these instances. The same rounding may shift the rate of 949 other instances by more than 1%. Since the tour and the packing used by the generator were not published, the original rates cannot be recovered. We therefore define new rates with the same rule, applied to a fully specified reference solution that we publish, and we show with a published heuristic that the corrected instances behave as TTP instances again. We also give reference results on the corrected instances and recommendations for future studies. Finally, we present ttplib, a new open-source Python library that loads any of the 9,720 instances, including the corrected ones, without downloading the whole benchmark.

</details>

Abstract: Ottoman Divan poetry is composed in aruz, a quantitative meter based on the weights of syllables. The meter is sensitive to specific characters recorded in the original writing in the Arabic script. This work is concerned with automatic meter finding and scansion alignment for Ottoman Divan poetry, especially on modern Turkish spelling without the special diacritics that scholarly transliterations of Ottoman preserve. We define two tasks on this input, first identifying the meter used in the poem, and second aligning the meter's weights with the syllabification of the poem, identifying the flaws and freedoms the poet has taken along the way. We build a corpus of 52,000 lines split by poet, with scansion annotations for 500 test lines, and a robustness benchmark generated at the deviation rates measured on real web and OCR copies. Our model is a hidden Markov model over the positions of each meter, decoded over a lattice of every candidate path, and trained by EM from the printed meter, without scansion labels. On a held-out set it scans 77.6% of the test lines fully correctly, against 26.8% for the available rule-based scanner, 55.0% for a neural tagger trained with the same supervision and 63.6% for Claude Opus 5.5 few-shot. The code is open-sourced and the tool is released on the web.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly mediate how people seek and consume information, including accounts that differ across languages and regions, especially on contested topics. Supporting diverse information seeking requires models to help users navigate competing narratives across languages and information streams. We define six capabilities necessary for diverse multilingual information seeking: three foundational capabilities for narrative inclusion, narrative exclusion, and missing narrative identification; and three higher-level capabilities for presenting diverse narratives, preserving narrative coverage under confirmatory queries, and maintaining information parity across languages. To guide the development of those information seeking capabilities, we introduce \textsc{NarrativeBench}, a benchmark comprising 1,333 news articles in 23 publication languages across 23 contested topics and 460 Reddit-grounded queries. Evaluating 14 LLMs across 24 query languages, we find that current models lack the capabilities needed to reliably navigate diverse narratives, limiting users' control over the perspectives they receive and leaving substantial gaps in exposure to competing accounts even when diversity is explicitly requested. Information access also differs across query framings and languages, risking echo chambers, linguistic filter bubbles, and information disparities that weaken the common ground needed for democratic discourse. \textsc{NarrativeBench} provides a foundation for developing information systems that give users across languages and regions agency to navigate diverse narratives.

</details>

Abstract: Preference optimization has become a central approach for aligning large language models with human values, but its effectiveness depends heavily on the quality of preference annotations. In practice, preference data is often noisy due to annotation errors and ambiguity. Existing robust preference optimization methods primarily operate at the sequence level, implicitly treating entire responses as uniformly correct or incorrect. However, rejected responses may still contain informative reasoning steps, while preferred responses can include subtle errors. In this work, we propose \textbf{P}reference \textbf{O}ptimization via \textbf{R}obust \textbf{T}oken-level reweighting \textbf{(PORT)}, a fine-grained framework for robust alignment under noisy preferences. PORT performs token-level reweighting using the empirical cumulative distribution function (CDF) of token log-probabilities, yielding an efficient forward-pass-only proxy to gradient-norm penalization that selectively suppresses corrupted tokens without additional backward-pass computation. We provide theoretical analysis showing that PORT reduces the gradient bias from noisy preference labels by minimizing an upper bound on the bias term. Extensive experiments across diverse noise settings demonstrate that PORT consistently improves robustness and outperforms existing sequence-level baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/osu-srml/PORT

</details>

Abstract: Developing effective machine learning models requires complex manual tuning, while current Automated Machine Learning (AutoML) frameworks remain domain-specific and isolate model architecture, training hyperparameter, and data ratio optimization. Here, we bridge this gap by proposing a universal, large language model (LLM)-based AutoML framework that jointly optimizes these configurations for the first time without the need for task-specific fine-tuning. We propose NCode, a unified numerical encoding scheme that translates diverse AutoML design choices into universal discrete sequences and effectively reframes the optimization process as a unified ranking task. Trained solely on optimization trajectories derived from a limited, single-domain vision task, the proposed LLM-based searcher demonstrates impressive cross-task, cross-modality and joint search-space optimization generalizability, achieving state-of-the-art performance across image classification, image segmentation, speaker verification and text-to-image benchmarks. Crucially, it exhibits superior scalability in the large-scale training and joint optimization of a billion-parameter Diffusion Transformer, requiring 896 GPU-days and attaining a state-of-the-art GenEval score of 0.91. By leveraging reinforcement learning to instill Chain-of-Thought reasoning, we provide a systematic way to interpret the LLM-based searcher's internal decision-making mechanisms, revealing its optimization strategy shifted from the early-stage diversity-driven exploration to the late-stage anchor exploitation. Our proposed framework provides a scalable, generalizable, and interpretable pathway toward general-purpose open-world machine learning optimization.

Abstract: One hundred known errors were planted into 10 open-access psychology papers, and the modified papers were then run through frontier models and two commercial AI review tools. The best single system caught 71 of the 100 errors, while the worst caught 30. Pooling every system's output caught 93. Models are only partly correlated in the errors they find, which makes ensembling a big lever for finding issues in papers. Seven errors could not be caught by any system, and all seven were omissions: information deleted from a paper rather than mistakes inserted into it. Refine.ink contributes more unique catches than any other single system, though it is expensive on a per-error basis. False positives were not measured, and how this error distribution compares to the distribution of errors in real papers remains unknown. The papers, errors, model outputs, and full experiment log are public, so that others can build on this toward a comprehensive evaluation benchmark across disciplines.

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly deployed as multi-agent systems rather than as a standalone model operating within a single context. Recent agent harnesses allow the model to instantiate worker agents and to author the executable workflow that coordinates them, shifting the design of system structure from developers to the model at run time. The quality of this organization therefore constitutes a distinct capability, yet existing benchmarks either measure end-to-end task completion or evaluate coordination under structures specified in advance. We formalize this capability as workflow orchestration and introduce WorkflowArena, a benchmark of 145 long-horizon tasks spanning 8 professional domains, from software engineering and formal mathematics to legal analysis and 3D production. Each task specifies an initial workspace and an integrated deliverable composed of interdependent components, which is assessed by a hidden verifier. Agents determine autonomously whether and how to delegate, and all delegation is recorded, enabling joint evaluation of deliverable quality and the organization of the underlying work. Experiments with nine models show that no single configuration performs best across all domains and that systems exhibit distinct delegation strategies: at high effort, Claude models in Claude Code invoke workflows less frequently but with larger worker groups, whereas GPT models in Codex delegate more frequently in smaller units. When the executing model is held fixed on a challenging subset of tasks, workflows authored by Claude Opus 5.5 substantially improve weaker executors, indicating that workflow organization is itself a measurable determinant of task performance.

Abstract: Persona generalization occurs when fine-tuning a language model in one domain induces behavioral changes in others -- for example, the broadly unsafe behavior observed in emergent misalignment. We discover a surprising "on-the-line" phenomenon in persona generalization: in-domain and out-of-domain persona rates across various fine-tuning hyperparameters follow an approximately probit-linear relationship, and the specific probit-linear relationship is surprisingly shared even across model families and model sizes, including Qwen and Llama. Training choices that leave the set of trainable parameters unchanged, such as learning rate, weight decay, and training duration, largely move models along the same line, reducing transfer mainly by weakening in-domain learning. Changing which layers are fine-tuned, however, can change the slope. For unsafe behavior, fine-tuning only later layers yields shallower slopes, retaining strong in-domain learning while reducing out-of-domain misalignment. This "on-the-line" phenomenon unifies unexpected, isolated observations of persona generalization within a quantitative framework for predicting and controlling behavioral transfer.

</details>

Abstract: As multimodal agents accumulate increasingly long interaction histories, effectively retrieving and utilizing past information becomes critical for long-term interaction. Existing memory systems typically rely on semantic retrieval over compressed memories and expose predefined representations, which may miss relevant memories and overlook heterogeneous evidence needs across queries. We introduce \textbf{\hivemem}, a multimodal memory framework that interleaves \textbf{\textsc{H}}or\textbf{\textsc{i}}zontal Memory Expansion with \textbf{\textsc{Ve}}rtical Evidence Composition. For horizontal memory expansion, \hivemem organizes memory episodes into a relational graph using textual and visual anchors, expanding semantically retrieved seeds with related neighbors to recover complementary information. For vertical evidence composition, each retrieved memory retains multiple evidence forms, including compact summaries, captions, grounded visual views, raw images, and interaction history. A lightweight trainable selector independently composes an evidence subset for each retrieved memory, enabling query-adaptive utilization of heterogeneous multimodal information. This two-dimensional design jointly broadens memory coverage and adapts the evidence contributed by individual memories. Experiments across three multimodal memory benchmarks show that \hivemem consistently improves task performance across base models while reducing token consumption by about 90\% compared with the most efficient competing multimodal baselines.

Abstract: AI sycophancy, the tendency of large language models (LLMs) to flatter users and affirm their beliefs, can undermine accuracy and contribute to misinformation, dependence, and overconfidence. Although prior work has benchmarked sycophancy in released models and proposed mitigations, its origins during training remain poorly understood. Preference alignment methods such as RLHF and DPO are often blamed, but their effects are rarely compared with earlier training stages. We investigate sycophancy across the training pipelines of three model families and three benchmarks. We find that sycophancy is largely established by the end of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), rather than introduced by preference alignment. Across all nine model benchmark combinations, SFT checkpoints attain at least 92% of preference aligned models' sycophancy scores and at least 85% of final models' scores; on average, they are about as sycophantic as final models. Preference alignment increases sycophancy in only four of nine cases, by an average of 4.2 percentage points when it does. To probe the underlying causes, we isolate model representations associated with sycophancy using mechanistic interpretability and measure their activation on training datasets from each stage. SFT datasets show roughly the same average activation as other training data but contribute substantially more examples, and the highest-scoring individual datasets all come from SFT. Our findings suggest that addressing sycophancy requires interventions throughout the training pipeline, particularly in data selection and augmentation for pre-training and SFT.

</details>

Human reviewers showed higher ordinal than nominal agreement (α = 0.494), indicating that disagreements often occurred between neighboring severity levels. In contrast, selected LLMs demonstrated substantially higher inter-model consistency, with ordinal agreement (α) reaching 0.8336 under the baseline prompt. Proprietary models generally showed better agreement with expert annotations and greater stability than open-source models. GPT-5.6-Terra achieved the best overall performance, while the open-source Qwen2.5-7B showed the strongest performance in identifying Acute Crisis with Suicidal Ideation cases. However, higher model consistency did not necessarily correspond to agreement with human judgments or reliable detection of rare high-severity cases. Label-flipped demonstration experiments also revealed model-specific sensitivity to misleading in-context examples, while explanatory demonstrations did not consistently improve performance. Overall, LLMs show promise as scalable complements to expert annotation, but require careful evaluation before use in high-stakes mental health applications.

Abstract: LoRA offers a parameter-efficient adaptation strategy, supporting multi-user workloads via customized adapters on a shared backbone. However, serving concurrent requests with different adapters introduces substantial computation and memory-management overhead. We ask the research question: Do all requests need their adapters throughout the whole generation process? Our motivational study reveals that employing LoRA adapters at every decoding step is unnecessary, releasing them early has limited impact on accuracy while substantially accelerating generation. Although skipping adapters early offers theoretical workload reduction, translating these computational savings into concrete throughput gains requires tailored GPU kernels. We present LoRazor, a multi-LoRA serving framework that combines early adapter release with active-row aware GPU kernels. LoRazor determines when to permanently release a request's adapter and continue decoding with the backbone alone. By dynamically resizing LoRA kernel launches as adapters are released, LoRazor trims idle GPU blocks. Across two backbones and four tasks, LoRazor achieves up to 3.02x request throughput and 2.53x output-token throughput over vLLM multi-LoRA, with a mean relative task-accuracy decrease of 2.89%.

</details>

These capabilities enable cross-sectional, longitudinal, and experimental research on how children use and respond to LLMs and how AI design may shape trust, epistemic judgment, curiosity, and other aspects of development. AYO provides the infrastructure to generate evidence that guides safer AI systems, educational practices, and policies for children growing up with AI.

These results establish META-IR as an effective pathway toward building LLMs capable of sustained, adaptive safety alignment under dynamic and evolving threat environments.

Opposition dominated discourse volume rather than disapproval: against posts outnumbered favor posts 2.6:1 and generated 8.5 times more comments, and this gap widened significantly over 86 weeks. Topic modeling of opposition arguments resolved into four reproducible clusters, resource consumption and scarcity (42.8%), contested siting processes and secrecy (34.2%), political opposition to data centers (13%), and political and corporate accountability (8%). Engagement analysis revealed that posts with emotional or provocative political framing attracted the most engagement, while key technical concerns (e.g., water, power) were dispersed across lower-engagement comment threads. These findings give local leaders, developers, and policy makers a quantified, continuously updatable basis for prioritizing concerns and policy levers, supporting a shift from opposition to negotiated, verifiable partnership for data center development.

Abstract: We study homeopathic learning, a phenomenon where poisoned training data with little effect on its own can create a hidden backdoor once clean, contrasting data is added. In our experiments, a model fine-tuned only on a "poison" dataset associated with a trait T (such as speaking German or being misaligned) does not learn T. Surprisingly, adding similarly formatted "clean" data not associated with T causes the model to learn T. This effect requires that the poison data have an identifying "trigger" feature that is absent from the clean data. We demonstrate this effect in a variety of settings: subliminal learning of backdoor behaviors, inoculation prompting of reward hacking transcripts, generalization from archaic bird names, and an MNIST classifier with mislabeled data. We propose a simple regression model as an explanation. In the poison data, the task and trigger always co-occur, and regularization favors attributing T to the task. Clean data without the trigger causes T to be attributed to the trigger instead. Consequently, the same training examples can have little effect as part of one training set but create a misalignment backdoor in another. This finding exposes a potential limitation of safety methods that audit or filter training data in isolation.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have continuous deficiency in spatial reasoning tasks that require \emph{mental spatial transformation}, such as reasoning under hypothetical changes in viewpoint or position, known as perspective-taking tasks. Existing approaches either scale linguistic reasoning or augment VLMs with static geometric representations, but neither directly enables active transformation of the represented scene. World models offer this capability, yet existing systems typically communicate through synthesized RGB images, introducing a latent-to-pixel-to-latent bottleneck. We introduce \emph{WorldMind}, a framework for test-time scaling of spatial reasoning through latent world simulation. Given a query and observation, the VLM selects useful spatial transformations, while a world model executes them and exposes aligned intermediate latent features directly to the VLM. The resulting latent evidence is incorporated into subsequent reasoning steps, enabling iterative spatial simulation without repeatedly decoding imagined states into pixels. To optimize this interaction, WorldMind further employs process-verifiable reinforcement learning that rewards reasoning progress only when the associated action and latent transition are valid. WorldMind thus scales spatial reasoning by allocating additional inference-time computation to active world simulation rather than generating more language or pixels. Across VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench, and MindCube-Tiny, WorldMind achieves an average improvement of 16.0% over the base VLM and 8.3% over the strongest RGB-based world simulation.

</details>

Abstract: Existing LLM post-training quantization techniques determine precision before inference begins, yet the precision a model requires varies dynamically across prompts, layers, and inference phases. We present AutoEMatic, a full-stack co-design that adapts the precision of weights, activations, and the KV cache at runtime. A lightweight policy selects each layer's precision at every prefill and decode step from two runtime signals: the entropy of the next-token distribution and the magnitude of each layer's update to its hidden states. This runtime adaptation is made practical by AutoFP, a multi-scale floating-point format that derives 2-, 4-, and 6-bit precisions from a single NVFP4 base format. We realize AutoEMatic on two targets: GPU kernels that execute AutoFP on NVFP4 Tensor Cores, and an accelerator whose vector MAC datapath executes all AutoFP formats natively. Across reasoning and agentic benchmarks, AutoEMatic improves accuracy by up to 21.7% over NVFP4 and matches group-scaled FP6 accuracy on most benchmarks, using 4.9 bits per weight versus 4.5 for NVFP4. On GB300 GPUs, AutoEMatic retains approximately 90-98% of NVFP4 decode throughput across the evaluated configurations. Furthermore, the AutoEMatic accelerator reduces latency by 1.6-2.3x and energy by 7-23% compared to an accelerator with NVFP4-only datapath at iso-accuracy.

</details>

Abstract: Independent verification organizations (IVOs) have emerged as a popular, bipartisan solution for facilitating high-quality independent evaluations of AI technologies. But momentum is outpacing design: there is emerging consensus on the necessary operating conditions for robust IVOs, but little discourse on institutional and policy specifications to ensure those conditions are met. IVOs are currently envisioned primarily as a mechanism operating at the AI frontier and model level to evaluate catastrophic risks. Little work has yet been done on how to establish or enforce an IVO ecosystem that is equipped to handle myriad risks (i.e., beyond catastrophic ones) and operate at the system and application level across the deployment and use stages of the AI lifecycle. This paper argues that IVO design should ensure the capacity for evaluating AI models, systems, and applications in context for both frontier companies and enterprise companies & AI-adopting organizations. Additionally, this paper provides early considerations to advance IVO discourse from normative to operative, and from existential to everyday risks. Drawing from existing institutional constructs in financial services, education, public health, and science, we outline key considerations on whom IVOs serve, what access they require, how they interact with external governing structures, how they are funded, and how they fit into the wider assurance ecosystem. Much of the structure and knowledge necessary to holistically design IVOs and their ecosystem does not yet exist, even as companies rush to ink agreements with their chosen evaluators.

Abstract: Today's LLM-based agents are vulnerable to deception by users and other agents. Abstract: Medical ultrasound (US) enables real-time, non-invasive diagnosis, yet building US-based AI systems is limited by data scarcity, costly expert annotation, and limited cross-center generalizability. EvoDiff formulates sample selection as a generate-to-retrieve process, in which diffusion-based optimization in feature space guided by information gain produces synthetic probes, and only the real unlabeled samples retrieved by these probes are annotated and used for downstream training. Across six benchmarks covering fifteen datasets and 16,696 images, we evaluate EvoDiff on convolutional network and transformer-based foundation model. EvoDiff approaches fully supervised performance with substantially fewer labels, outperforms active learning baselines, and improves foundation model generalization under cross-domain external validation on unseen cohorts. Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly support Web-based services that must balance response quality and inference cost across heterogeneous requests. Abstract: Mathematical understanding depends on the route from hypotheses to conclusion: how the parts of a proof function and which dependencies carry the argument forward. This structure can persist when wording or notation changes, while a small alteration to a hypothesis or inference can produce a different argument beneath similar prose. Formal verification can establish that a rewrite proves the same theorem, but not whether it follows the same mathematical route. Comparing informal proofs, especially those produced or revised by generative models, therefore requires reconstructing which claims, dependencies, and reasoning steps are preserved, reorganized, or replaced.
  We present ProofDiff, a system for comparing informal mathematical documents. It is designed to support human review, not to certify mathematical correctness. Given two documents, ProofDiff independently constructs a source-grounded argument map for each and proposes correspondences between the resulting maps. Each map captures the document's claims and reasoning steps and records the typed relations that connect them. An integrated human-review layer allows reviewers to revise the maps and correspondences while preserving an auditable history. Comparing the two argument maps provides a basis for inspecting whether the documents follow the same mathematical route and where their claims or dependencies differ. We demonstrate ProofDiff on four illustrative pairs of AI-generated proofs, ranging from a detailed proof and its concise revision to proofs using different mathematical strategies.

</details>

Abstract: A phone screen can be wrong in ways that need no reference: a label that runs off the screen, letters that touch, a line that breaks one character before its end, one button with sharp corners among round ones. We ask whether a model can box every such fault on a screenshot, given no target design and no reference image. Such a critic could serve as a cheap first pass over generated pages before a designer reviews them, but only if it stays silent on clean pages. ScreenProof poses this target-free critique over three families of faults: general faults, local style faults (one element breaks the style its page sets) and text layout faults (letter spacing, line height, line breaks). A data engine makes each fault in page code and records the changed element's box, and hard negatives change every sibling alike. No frontier judge is reliable: frontier models flag 18-62% of clean real screenshots and 18-82% of clean generated pages, find 47-72% of text layout faults, and rarely find a foreign icon (5-19%). Our small critic ProofJudge (Qwen3.5-9B with LoRA), trained on code-made pages with one to four faults each and keeping a finding only when it is confident, reaches F1 0.48, the highest of all judges (Gemini 4 Argon 0.37; open models at most 0.15; 0.46 even without the confidence cut), and is the only fault-finding judge within 15% false alarms on clean pages (2% real, 9% generated), the property such a first pass needs, at about a hundredth of Argon's cost. It finds the most general faults (59%) but fewer local style faults than Argon (25% against 41%) and fewer text layout faults than the API judges (43%; Gemini 4 Argon 47%). Against a named brand the task is harder still: models know a design language about as well as its public documentation states it.

Abstract: The phrase recursive self-improvement (RSI) refers to the idea of an AI system designing and creating another, better AI system, which in turn designs and creates an even better one, and so on. While the idea has a long history, it has always been an abstract, futuristic idea -- until recently. Today's frontier AI has attained a set of abilities, including in programming, mathematics, research, tool use, and working coherently over extended periods of time, that make RSI seem plausible. AI companies are starting to pursue it, but many of the involved people are also expressing concerns, asking whether humanity should really set this in motion and under what conditions. I will discuss the situation, argue that we do not even have clear frameworks for thinking about the likely outcomes of RSI, and do a quick game-theoretic analysis of how to choose whether to initiate RSI when another party is making the same choice.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used to support decisions that require moral consideration. Abstract: Knowledge graphs need validated relational triples, and gold benchmarks of such triples are often built by several annotators. Most text annotation platforms focus on span tagging and compute agreement on categorical labels. They are not designed for ordinal quality grading of triples that have already been extracted. We present TripleGrader, an open-source web platform for grading subject-predicate-object triples on a 0 to 10 ordinal scale. In TripleGrader, each triple is scored independently, and peer scores stay hidden until the grader has rated it. Graders then discuss entries in threaded comments and propose revisions, which the session owner approves and publishes as new versions. For agreement, the platform computes quadratic-weighted Cohen's kappa for two raters and a quadratic-weighted Fleiss-type kappa for three or more. Graded sessions and the final benchmark are exported in common formats. We evaluated the platform in three ways. We ran 72 automated tests, including 18 workflow scenarios through the REST API. We also injected 10 error types to check whether prescribed corrections could be applied while preserving unaffected triples. On two small corpora with existing gold benchmarks, we then used the platform to curate the triples extracted by four large language models into each benchmark. Only 10% to 32% of the candidate triples could be kept unchanged, and 53% to 71% needed corrections, partly to match the benchmarks' naming conventions. Per model, 11 to 29 missing triples had to be added. On these corpora, the extracted triples therefore needed substantial human review. Abstract: LLM agents increasingly drive systems research and rely on simulators to evaluate candidate designs at scale. Simulators are imperfect by design: their modeling limitations can distort predictions yet leave the recommended design correct, or change which design should be selected. We introduce Siris to test whether LLMs can tell these cases apart, judging from their systems understanding when a limitation invalidates the simulator's recommendation. By perturbing a simulator, Siris constructs workload pairs with the same candidates, limitation, and simulator recommendation. The perturbation changes predictions on both workloads, yet a reference simulator supports the recommendation on only one. Evaluating thirteen frontier LLMs on LLM serving design problems, we find that identifying a limitation does not ensure a correct choice, and even the strongest models sometimes abandon valid recommendations. Self-correction through a review turn helps these models restore valid choices but introduces new errors in several others. These findings motivate evaluating systems understanding through design decisions under imperfect simulators.

Abstract: Feature caching accelerates Diffusion Transformers by reusing intermediate computations across denoising timesteps, yet existing methods share a fundamental blind spot: they treat the standard reference denoising trajectory as the optimisation target, approximating its per-step states and thus inheriting its output as an implicit quality ceiling. We argue that this target is arbitrary -- what truly matters is the ideal result, not the intermediate path taken to reach it. Instead, it anchors caching on a per-input ensemble of ideal trajectories constructed from candidate target results, aggregates their geometry into a single graph, and discovers optimal non-uniform schedules via a constrained shortest path on this graph. By decoupling from the reference process, IdealCache breaks the performance bottleneck of trajectory-approximation; our schedules can attain near-lossless fidelity in generation and even surpass on task-specific metrics (e.g., maintaining higher semantic consistency in image editing). Abstract: Accurate indoor localization on resource-constrained edge devices remains challenging due to the tension between modeling fidelity and computational efficiency. Wi-Fi Channel State Information (CSI) enables low-cost deployment using existing Wi-Fi infrastructure but is heavily distorted by multipath propagation. Transformer-based solutions mitigate these effects but their quadratic complexity limits real-time edge deployment. Ultra-Wideband (UWB) provides geometrically precise ranging yet requires dedicated infrastructure. We present MambaLoc, a cross-modal distillation framework that improves the accuracy-efficiency balance in CSI-based localization. MambaLoc uses a linear-time Selective State Space Model (Mamba) as a lightweight CSI student, guided during training by a UWB-Transformer teacher. A Feature Alignment Module bridges the representational gap between heterogeneous RF modalities, allowing the CSI model to inherit UWB-derived geometric structure without UWB hardware at inference. A probabilistic Gaussian Mixture regression head models multi-modal spatial ambiguity from multipath effects, avoiding the physically implausible averaging of deterministic regression. Evaluations on a real-world dataset show MambaLoc achieves a median localization error of 0.230 m, reducing error by 22.2% over a standalone Mamba baseline, while requiring 65.1% fewer FLOPs and 58% lower inference latency than Transformer-based approaches.

Abstract: Most temporal graph learning methods reduce future prediction to discriminative inference over past interactions, focusing on edges or node labels for a largely persistent set of nodes. This view breaks down when new nodes and edges appear, since the future graph becomes a new object with changing size, composition, and structure. We address this gap by framing temporal graph prediction as an inverse topology problem. Instead of predicting edges directly, we first predict a multiscale topological descriptor of the future graph and then reconstruct a plausible future snapshot that realizes this descriptor under inductive constraints. This approach makes global structure and node churn explicit prediction targets and produces forecasted graphs that downstream tasks can evaluate without retraining. Across 14 temporal graph datasets, we evaluate our method, TOPOGED, on node, link, and graph property prediction and compare it against state-of-the-art temporal graph models. TOPOGED significantly improves node-level forecasting accuracy over the strongest baseline, with a macro average of 0.49 versus 0.05. It also outperforms baselines in 60% of graph-structure metric evaluations and substantially increases macro-average edge prediction metrics, from near-zero to 0.12. Our results show that topology-guided graph forecasting can predict inductive future snapshots whose structure supports multiple downstream evaluations.

Abstract: When disasters strike, they not only damage infrastructure, buildings, and the economy, but also affect the mental health of affected populations. Research on the mental health effects of disasters has expanded rapidly and has become an important, well-funded area of psychology, public health, and related social sciences. However, the overall structure of this research landscape has not been systematically characterized. Using large language models, we conducted a review of all articles related to terms 'disaster' and 'mental health' indexed in PubMed. 7,859 articles' metadata were retrieved using the NCBI API, and then Claude Sonnet was used to extract structured variables from the abstracts. The results indicated that earthquakes and epidemics were the most commonly studied disasters while many other disasters such as heat and cold waves have received less attention. PTSD, depression, and anxiety were well investigated but many other mental health issues (e.g., aggression, suicide) are relatively ignored. Despite enjoying relatively large sample sizes (median = 452, IQR = 142 - 1,568), the field rarely utilizes machine learning algorithms. Hypothesis significance testing, reviews, qualitative methods, and structural equation modeling are respectively most common methods while agent-based modeling, machine learning, casual inference, and NLP each appear in less than 1% of articles. Studies are limited to a small number of countries, particularly the United States, Japan, and China, while many disaster-prone regions remain substantially underrepresented. This LLM-assisted computational map provides a broad overview of the disaster mental health literature while highlighting important methodological gaps, with particular emphasis on the underutilization of computational methods that could help advance the field.

</details>

Abstract: Big techs and researchers increasingly claim that their training datasets are too large to inspect for bias and ethical problems. However, in the race to build ever-large models, they have relied on automated web scraping and no human curation. Datasets built this way absorb the stereotypes, exclusionary norms, and historical prejudices of their sources, and the models trained on them can reproduce and amplify those patterns. Because manually auditing billions of samples is humanly impossible, we need practical, effective methods to identify biases in these datasets. This paper studies the feasibility of ethically auditing large datasets through small samples and proposes a practical method for identifying structural biases by applying AI Ethics Tools (AIETs) to random samples. We applied Datasheets for Datasets, Data Statements, Data Nutrition Labels, and Data Cards to a 1% sample of Framed Multi30K, and Datasheets for Datasets to two 0.00006% samples of relaion2B-en-research-safe. Our results show that evaluating small samples with AIETs can reveal ethical problems, structural biases, and technical inconsistencies. Although industry practice assumes that identifying problems requires analyzing the entire dataset, we show that a random subset supports a viable qualitative analysis without overwhelming computational or human resources. Demonstrating that a dataset is biased does not require examining every instance: if a bias appears in a random sample, it is a flaw of the creation process. The AIETs' questions acted as provocateurs, evoking different perspectives and analysis methods for the same dataset, but the analysis and its results depended fundamentally on the human factor. We hope these findings contribute to urgent public discussions about ethics, transparency, accountability, and AI regulation by offering a practical pathway for responsibly auditing massive datasets.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based agents extend LLMs with tool calls, memory, planning, and retrieval to act in external environments, but they are vulnerable to attacks at different workflow steps. Existing defenses, including prompt engineering and post-training alignment, primarily improve LLM-only safety and often overlook risks that arise from multi-step execution. External verification adds guardrails to check each step separately, but it typically ignores how risks can propagate across steps. We argue that we should consider risk transitions across dependent steps through the agent workflow, rather than separate verification on a single step. In this paper, we propose \textbf{T}ail-risk-aware \textbf{A}gent \textbf{S}afety (TAS), a tail-risk-aware safety framework that models agent workflow safety as a sequence of dependent safety states and constrains the rare but extreme risks (i.e., tail risk) with a shared budget. TAS first models safety-state transitions sequentially along the workflow as an MDP to trace risk propagation, then measures a tail-sensitive risk to target rare but high-cost risks (i.e., tail risk), and constrains the risk with a shared budget to derive a step-wise safety strategy. By constraining tail risk rather than the overall average, TAS suppresses rare high-cost failures while preserving utility in normal cases. Experiments show that TAS achieves better performance compared with eleven baselines across three benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed through conversational interfaces, yet strong single-turn performance does not imply reliability in multi-turn settings. Multi-turn assistance requires a model to maintain an evolving representation of user requirements and to reason over it before complying with later requests. Failures can therefore be subtle: models may fail to recognize a changed constraint or hidden conflict, or recognize it without enforcing it in the final response. Existing benchmarks primarily test fully specified prompts, long-context recall, or controlled domain environments, leaving open-domain requirement reconciliation undermeasured. We introduce TurnFlow, an interactive benchmark for evaluating whether models preserve, revise, and act on user requirements across turns. TurnFlow combines adaptive user simulation with verifiable judging, allowing conversations to unfold naturally while keeping hidden requirements and pass/fail criteria controlled. In our evaluation, nine LLMs achieve overall pass rates from 42.86% to 59.59% on 490 scenarios. Performance varies substantially across challenge types: models perform well on Stateful Multi-Constraint Following but remain considerably less reliable on Scope Creep and Goal Shift. These results reveal persistent limitations in reasoning over evolving user requirements across turns.

Abstract: Semantic response caching reuses a cached response for a semantically similar query to improve LLM inference efficiency. However, query similarity alone can lead to unsafe reuse when safety conditions change. Existing approaches improve cache safety through stricter similarity checks, context-aware validation, or response verification, yet they may still miss safety conflicts between the cached response and the new request or introduce unnecessary verification overhead. We argue that safe and efficient semantic caching requires a reuse policy that makes the cached response's safety conditions explicit and uses them to guide the reuse decision with a balance between safety and utility. In this paper, we propose SafeCache, a safety-aware framework that separates candidate retrieval from safety approval and formulates semantic response reuse as a safety-conditioned routing problem. SafeCache matches each cached-response condition against the new request and aggregates the per-condition mismatches into an overall risk score. A separate contradiction guard prevents explicit conflicts from being diluted by strong matches. It further estimates matching uncertainty and calibrates the routing policy under a required unsafe-reuse target and a verifier-call budget to choose among direct reuse, selective verification, or regeneration. Experiments show that SafeCache achieves the better safety-utility trade-off among the evaluated baselines, enabling safer response reuse at comparable cache utility.

</details>

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) rollouts interleave model reasoning with tool calls in persistent workspaces. Scaling these rollouts incurs substantial memory costs for concurrent environments, while slow tool calls delay entire rollout batches. We present BunshinRL, an agentic RL rollout system that exploits shared environment state and recurring tool calls through three techniques: memory sharing across environments, tool-prefix caching, and speculative tool execution. These techniques build on BunshinFork, a novel live whole-workspace forking mechanism for containers. BunshinFork reconstructs process trees, shares memory copy-on-write, and preserves file, communication, and GUI state, enabling cached-state reuse and rollback after incorrect speculation. We implement BunshinRL and BunshinFork through Linux kernel module and CRIU modifications. BunshinRL reduces peak memory by up to 43%, total memory consumption by up to 51%, and end-to-end rollout latency by 23%.

Abstract: We study decentralised drone formation, without a central planner, under the conditions that make it hard: nobody knows how many drones are alive, failures are silent, radio is short-ranged and lossy, and actuation is noisy. In our controller, ant colony optimisation (ACO) allocates drones to picture positions through claims that behave as reinforced, evaporating pheromone and are gossiped between neighbours, so evaporation doubles as failure detection. A particle swarm optimisation (PSO) law flies the drones, a noise-buffered Voronoi filter keeps them apart, and PSO also tunes the controller offline. We prove that claims of destroyed drones vanish within bounded time, that conflicts between drones that exchange tables are resolved consistently, that survivors cover the picture to within a factor of two, and that drones cannot collide while their safety cells are feasible. On held-out seeds and with two tuned configurations, the controller formed the picture in 95-99% of runs in the hardest regime, restored it after a silent loss of half the swarm in 96-100% of runs at normal difficulty, transferred unchanged to eight unseen shapes and to 300 drones, and overlapped in 5 of 21,088 runs. A CBBA-style baseline that shares the evaporating claims but uses argmax without swaps was at least as reliable as ours once tuned with the same budget, and repaired faster, whereas ours generalised better to unseen shapes. The benefit thus comes from the pheromone dynamics and from tuning, not from ACO's stochastic construction or PSO's social terms. A centralised oracle is faster.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents need to choose actions whose consequences unfold over multiple future steps, yet poor decisions do not reveal whether the model failed to represent those consequences or failed to use them. Mechanistic interpretability has studied internal representations of environment state and plans, but sequential decision making also requires evaluating how candidate actions change future outcomes. Reinforcement learning formalizes this as action value. We study this computation in Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, and GPT-OSS-20B across a partially observed Grid environment and WebShop, a simulated web shopping environment. We find that the relative values of candidate actions are linearly decodable from intermediate hidden states, including at states where the policy prefers a worse action. We call this gap between value representation and policy choice value-policy dissociation. To investigate how represented value relates to action selection, we compare direct interventions along the value readout direction with Natural Value Transport (NVT), a transformation learned from hidden state changes that naturally result from differences in action value. While readout-direction interventions have limited and inconsistent effects, NVT more consistently influences action preferences, particularly at later layers. We also test a closed-loop controller that uses the value readout as a sensor and NVT as an actuator, observing improvements in task outcomes. These results suggest that LLM agents can represent useful prospective value without reliably translating it into policy choice.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning-trained agents for retrieval-augmented generation retrieve content (passages, knowledge-graph fragments, or hyperedges) and supervise training with outcome rewards on the final answer. Across these paradigms the reasoning route through retrieved content never leaves the language model's hidden state, conflating declarative knowledge with the procedural knowledge that recurs across queries of the same logical type; content-only methods consequently plateau as reasoning depth grows.
