A first RL stage scores the agent's constructed evidence graph by a frozen oracle's information gain through the Information-Density Reward (IDR), a size-normalized mutual-information surrogate. High-reward trajectories are distilled into a Graph Pattern Memory (GPM) of type-abstracted reasoning skeletons, and a second RL stage uses the Pattern-Augmented Reward (PAR) to couple policy to memory, turning procedural reasoning into a learnable, externalized structural prior. A 7B PatternBloom outperforms $27$ baselines by $+15.0$ Avg-OOD EM, with the gain compounding with reasoning depth precisely because procedural knowledge, once distilled, is reused rather than re-derived at every query. Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly used for open-ended tasks, very common in research scenarios where they are required to explore, investigate, or act curiously. At inference time, an LLM is a fixed map from context to next-token distribution, the pretrained weights determine the function, and only its conditioning changes as context accumulates. Mechanisms like temperature, best-of-N, tree search, or RAG vary how the distribution is sampled or what it is conditioned on, but do not update the agent's model of the environment. We argue that current LLM agents do not explore curiously; they explore stochastically. From how curiosity is understood in the RL literature, it requires a predictive structure over how the environment responds to the agent's actions, which improves online from current interaction, drives action selection through its predicted improvement, and persists beyond the context window. Additionally, in its open-ended form, the agent should intrinsically originate what to investigate. Thus, we propose a framework defining curiosity in LLM agents, reserving the term for systems that satisfy it. In doing so, we identify the research directions its implementation opens up.

</details>

Abstract: Large-scale retail supply chains need sales forecasts for specified products, future dates or planning windows, and regions under the conditions available at the forecast origin. Regional sparsity, changing environments, volatile sales, and unfamiliar business situations make these dimensions difficult to model together. We introduce TimeHF 2.0, a multimodal, multivariate, and multi-region collaborative forecasting framework that makes flexibly expressed business knowledge usable in numerical prediction. A coverage-aware directed encoder integrates regional sales and applicable covariates. A semantic alignment module transfers language-model hidden representations of natural-language plans and domain experience into target-specific forecasting context. Learning against sales observations connects these meanings to numerical evidence, allowing business users to supply experience, future plans, and changing circumstances without defining a new numerical feature for each description. Text-conditioned future queries and shared/routed experts adapt prediction to the supplied context. An ordered-distribution head allocates fine resolution to routine fluctuations and coarse resolution to extreme sales movements, while daily/window learning connects forecasts to cumulative planning quantities. Online parallel forecasting at JD yields relative accuracy improvements of 18.25% and 17.94% for national daily and planning-window sales, and 19.44% and 18.99% for DC daily and planning-window sales, over the original production deep learning baseline. Component comparisons, loss-design results, and product-type analyses examine the roles of regional information, covariates, business knowledge, context-dependent processing, and robust planning-oriented learning.

Abstract: Today's large language models (LLMs) increasingly interact with time: AI agents schedule tasks, coordinate with people and other AI systems, and even decide how long to think. Yet LLMs only ever encounter time secondhand---in descriptions rather than experience. Does this hinder their reasoning and decisions that involve time? Our paper traces this question from a model's knowledge to its actions through four successive evaluations. We find that models know the rules of time, which can be read, but struggle to predict durations, which must be lived; while they are highly accurate (92.0-97.5\%) on a collection of questions built from the theorems of various time-related literatures, they underestimate their own completion times by up to 99.5\% and fail to identify which tasks will take them longer to run. And while they are better at estimating durations of human tasks, their duration judgments change based on how an event is worded, magnifying similar human biases by up to 1.3$\times$. Moving from estimation to action, we find that some LLMs disregard time costs completely when answering user queries, ignoring a 1,200-fold increase in wait time entirely. Together, our experiments reveal new challenges posed to LLMs as they navigate a dimension they cannot directly perceive, and highlight how their blind spots can even be simple cognitive abilities that humans take for granted.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inherit strong textual reasoning capabilities from their language backbones and text pretraining, but often fail to apply the same level of competency to visual inputs. We show that this degradation cannot be attributed to only perception failures, by evaluating models on semantically-equivalent text and rendered-text image inputs: models can accurately read text in the images and recover near-text performance when provided their own transcriptions, but just cannot reason well on the images directly. We characterize this discrepancy as a modality reasoning-access gap, where models fail to route perceived visual content into existing reasoning machinery accessible with text. To bridge this, we propose TIGER (Text-to-Image Gap-targeted training for Enhanced Reasoning), which first mines synthetic modality counterfactuals where a model succeeds on a text problem but fails when provided the corresponding rendered image, and post-trains the model on the image input via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), along with SFT and DPO variants. Importantly, although trained exclusively on synthetic rendered-text images with no multimodal annotations, TIGER results in model performance improvements across diverse real-world visual benchmarks including geometric diagrams (MathVerse), multi-disciplinary sciences (EMMA, MMMU), complex charts (ChartQAPro), and embodied spatial reasoning (RoboSpatial-Home). TIGER also complements existing state-of-the-art RLVR visual reasoning methods, yielding further performance gains. Analysis involving reasoning-subspace activation and activation patching provide support indicating that TIGER helps to align visual-to-reasoning pathways toward text reasoning states. Abstract: Angle Power Spectrum (APS) and Power Delay Profile (PDP) are receiver-facing spectra that govern beam selection and wideband equalization. Most ray-tracing benchmarks provide supervision as variable-size multipath component lists. This format does not match practical receivers, whose angular and delay resolutions are limited by array aperture and system bandwidth. As a result, learning targets are ambiguous and evaluations can change with reconstruction conventions. We introduce UrbanADPSMap, a benchmark that converts ray-traced supervision into fixed-grid APS and PDP targets by explicitly modeling bandwidth-limited and aperture-limited response kernels. By fixing the receiver-domain kernels, discretization, and validity mask, UrbanADPSMap removes reconstruction-induced target ambiguity and stabilizes downstream evaluation across methods. Built on this benchmark, we propose SpecFlowAR, a coarse-to-fine generator based on multi-scale autoregressive flow matching. The model first captures coarse dispersion structure and then refines beam-relevant peaks and low-power tails. The number of function evaluations controls the sampling budget, enabling latency and quality to be adjusted for dense spectrum queries. Experiments on the test set show that SpecFlowAR reduces BPL@5 to 2.03 dB under a unified NFE=80 budget, while improving beam utility and reducing errors of 3GPP dispersion statistics.

</details>

Existing agent benchmarks do not directly measure such higher-level errors. We introduce InfoOrgBench, a benchmark of 150 tasks in which agents organize scattered records from meeting recaps, issue threads and design proposals into hierarchies. These results show that evaluating information organization requires measuring higher-level relations separately from record preservation and local grouping.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) inference serving is undergoing rapid growth and large-scale deployment, motivating us to rethink how the inference process itself can be leveraged to enable efficient task-specific LLM adaptation. In this paper, we propose a forward-only approach for efficient LLM adaptation with forward-only passes in LLM inference serving. We exploit the angle concentration of activations induced by each singular value decomposition (SVD) component to measure its contribution-dispersed or concentrated-to the angle concentration of the hidden states. Based on this, our forward-only search then efficiently identifies the weight matrix with the highest dispersion that merits rank reduction. We then selectively remove the higher-order components and retain the lower-order components in SVD. Empirical results across diverse datasets demonstrate the competitive accuracy of our forward-only approach, while theoretical analysis shows lower peak memory and greater speedup than the gradient-based approach and greater speedup than the exhaustive search approach. The experiments further present the robustness and generalization of our forward-only approach to various LLMs with up to 57B parameters. The code is included in the supplementary material.

Abstract: When an LLM agent's backend model is upgraded or replaced, operators typically reload the same persona, memory, and rules onto the new model and assume the agent will behave as before. We show this assumption requires empirical validation-and that whether identity is preserved depends on the specific model pairing. Across 5,760 compliance-testing runs in 12 regulated domains with three open-weight models, we find that some transitions produce significant behavioral drift while others do not, even when the agent's external state remains identical. Crucially, this drift is invisible to verbal verification: every model claims full policy compliance, yet actual decisions under pressure differ across models. The same external state can reorder which model is most compliant-a large positive effect on one substrate, negligible on the other two. Practitioners should treat model transitions as behavioral changes requiring action-level empirical testing, not as transparent infrastructure operations.

</details>

This benchmark study builds four offloading systems small enough to solve exactly by dynamic programming and measures reward and learner against the optimum. A bonus for a met limit makes each miss cost its penalty plus the bonus, which shifts the class weights of published per-class rewards. A class-blind penalty stays 65% to 118% above the optimal cost even with a perfect learner, while a corrected per-class penalty comes within 0.13% of the optimum. With a per-class reward, tuned proximal policy optimization (PPO) agents come within 9% to 11% of the optimum in the smallest system, and the corrected one beats a tuned reservation rule in 9 of 10 seeds. In larger systems the agents stay 41% to 76% above the optimum, and a tuned rule beats their mean. Training ten times longer does not close this gap, and a PPO-Lagrangian agent with reachable targets does no better than the per-class reward.

Abstract: Cellular world models aim to predict how cells respond to genetic perturbations, analogous to world models in reinforcement learning. Perturbation response models are evaluated almost exclusively by global Pearson correlation between predicted and observed transcriptomes. We show this metric is dominated by thousands of non-responsive genes and severely inflates apparent performance. On the Replogle K562 screen (509 unseen perturbations, 5000 genes), GEARS achieves global Pearson 0.977, but its delta Pearson is only 0.076. A trivial baseline predicting mean training delta achieves 0.267, which is 3.5x better than GEARS. Our primary contribution is methodological: we identify and quantify this delta gap as a systematic evaluation artifact, and propose delta-space evaluation as the primary benchmark. We provide two post-hoc corrections (RWR on co-expression graphs and kNN matching) and show that 2.8x of the remaining gap lies in perturbation representation geometry, not decoder capacity. We further show the gap is partially addressable end-to-end: delta-aligned training (lambda = 3.0) raises delta Pearson to 0.179 on Replogle (2.35x over GEARS) and surpasses the trivial baseline on direction accuracy (0.680 vs 0.567) and gene recovery (1.52 vs 1.19); combined with kNN it reaches 0.217. An amplitude-calibration variant (eta = 0.3) further raises gene recovery to 2.16 while reaching 0.218. Critically, these representations transfer zero-shot across cell lines: from Replogle K562 to unseen Norman A549, the delta-aligned model reaches 0.328 on 741 shared genes, 4.7x a trivial baseline and 2.1x standard GEARS. This is the first systematic quantification of the delta gap, and the first diagnostic framework localizing the residual gap to perturbation representation geometry via an oracle upper bound.

</details>

Abstract: Document parsing benchmarks are often treated as stable measures of model quality, yet rankings may depend substantially on benchmark design choices. In this work, we present RankShift, a systematic study comparing OCR and vision-language models (VLMs) under controlled variation in dataset composition, matching strategy, and scoring methodology. Using OmniDocBench as a case study, we evaluate the ranking stability of 16 models across 3 dataset configurations, 6 matching strategies, and 3 scoring metrics. We quantify rank stability using pairwise rank correlation, top-k churn, and pairwise reversal rate. Our results show that the interaction between matching strategy and ground truth format is a substantial but understudied driver of instability: scoring against OmniDocBench's JSON annotations versus its Markdown rendering yields a mean Kendall's tau of approximately 0.33 under region-level matching strategies, with individual pairs as low as tau = 0.23, compared with tau = 0.77 when each page is scored as a single concatenated string. Dataset composition produces comparable instability across page-type strata (mean tau = 0.48) and layout-type strata (mean tau = 0.49), while the cross-dataset shift from OmniDocBench to Real5's Scanning reconstruction of the same pages is more stable (tau = 0.80). Metric choice is the most stable dimension, with mean tau of approximately 0.78 across NED, BLEU, and METEOR. These findings indicate that leaderboard position is not solely a property of model capability, motivating leaderboards that report not only headline scores but also the robustness of rankings across reasonable design choices.

</details>

Abstract: Pre-training is where every foundation model begins; bringing recursive self-improvement (RSI) there would let AI improve the process that creates AI. Yet public RSI work has so far targeted only post-training, which starts from an existing base model, runs trials costing GPU-hours, and returns verifiable scores. Pre-training breaks all three conditions at orders-of-magnitude higher cost: a run consumes hundreds of thousands of GPU-hours over months and cannot be repeated; its objective, post-training headroom, cannot be read off any single score; and what must improve is not a model but the whole production system, from data pipelines and training infrastructure to the teams that run them. To our knowledge, we are the first to apply RSI to an entire frontier pre-training run from scratch and to report it in detail. RSI does not stop at frontier pre-training, but it changes form: since no end-to-end loop can close over a single unrepeatable run, we decompose it into smaller loops that can, each repairing a step the full run breaks. The result is 801-30B-A3B-Base, a 30B-A3B MoE trained from scratch on 11.8T tokens in three months by a newly formed team. We release it as a base model; the lightweight SFT reported here (6B tokens, no RL) only probes post-training headroom. As a base model, 801-30B-A3B outperforms Qwen3-30B-A3B and Nemotron-3.5-Lightning-30B-A3B, trailing only the larger Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, and keeps this position under identical SFT; with lightweight SFT alone, it achieves the highest average on four agentic benchmarks against fully post-trained models, including Qwen3.6-35B-A3B. Since post-training RSI presupposes a strong base model, bringing RSI into pre-training acts on the foundation every later stage builds on. We open-source 801-30B-A3B-Base and the lightweight SFT data at https://github.com/GAIR-NLP/801-30B .

Abstract: A brain is in one state at a time, yet most of what decides its next state is absent from it: the patterns its world never produces, the statistics of a lifetime, a goal that lies ahead. In mechanics a wire acts on a bead only through a force that appears when the bead presses on it, and Lagrange computed such forces with one multiplier for each constraint. We propose that a brain follows the same mechanics. Everything a circuit knows, is told or wants is a constraint on its single state and acts through its own multiplier, which accumulates the violation and pushes the state back. Neurons and synapses carry the multipliers, each with its own time constant: inhibitory interneurons carry what never happens, adaptation the cost of staying in one state, synapses the statistics of experience, and feedback to apical dendrites the future. From this hypothesis follow Dale's law, the reciprocal wiring and balance of excitation and inhibition, the paradoxical responses of cortex and the frequencies of brain rhythms. Belief becomes the time one trajectory spends in each state, learning becomes sleep proposing laws and waking refuting the false ones, and planning becomes a wave from the goal meeting a wave from the present. Connectionism and symbolism are the limits of frozen and of instantaneous multipliers, and between them the time a circuit runs becomes the depth of its computation. A machine built on these principles, trained without gradients on 1000 solved puzzles, solves all 422,786 Sudoku-Extreme test puzzles with one trajectory each. It rediscovers the rules of Sudoku by sleeping and waking, plans shortest paths in mazes of 64 times the training area, and tracks permutations exactly on strings 25 times longer than in training, where Transformers fail. A mind, on this view, is life that learns its own constraints.

</details>

Abstract: Stroke is a disorder of the circulation, yet the haemodynamic state that immediately precedes it has rarely been observed, because continuous physiological recordings before a stroke are seldom available. Photoplethysmography (PPG), recorded by every pulse oximeter and by hundreds of millions of wearable devices, captures the peripheral pulse beat by beat, but its morphology is largely discarded. Here we ask whether pulse waveform morphology encodes haemodynamic signatures of impending stroke. Using in-hospital stroke as a natural experiment that provides continuous PPG before physician-adjudicated stroke recognition, we studied intensive-care (MIMIC-III, $n=176$) and emergency-department (MC-MED, $n=154$) cohorts and described each beat with interpretable descriptors of pulse timing, amplitude, contour and variability. At a nominal 360-min horizon, morphology separated pre-recognition from stable windows with $F_1$ scores of 0.941 and 0.989 (sensitivity 0.998 and 0.980), with models transferred unchanged to the second cohort, and outperformed heart rate alone on identical windows. The signature was dominated by systolic crest time and its change from each patient's own baseline, feature attribution was concordant across cohorts (Spearman $ρ=0.865$), and morphology recorded in the first two hours of care stratified the subsequent clinical course ($F_1$ 0.714 and 0.691). In high-risk patients without stroke, 29% and 14% of windows were positive, but five-window persistence rules limited alerts to 17% and 2% of patients. The results demonstrate that the routinely recorded pulse thus carries haemodynamic imprint of the period preceding stroke, positioning pulse morphology as a candidate continuous cerebrovascular biomarker.

Abstract: Search allows a robotic policy to evaluate candidate futures before committing to an action. Motivated by test-time planning for vision-language action (VLA) models, we study selective search over action chunks in PushT using a frozen Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) policy. Beam search generates perturbed alternatives around the policy prediction and scores them through rollouts from cloned simulator states. Across 50 evaluation episodes, beam search raises task success from 0% to 18% and increases average cumulative reward from 39.44 to 103.2. We introduce One Step Ahead (OSA), a gate that simulates the nominal policy chunk and invokes full search only when its predicted coverage drop exceeds a threshold. In the reported ablations, OSA preserves or improves success while reducing runtime by approximately 3x relative to full beam search. Ablations over tree depth, branching factor, beam width, and replanning frequency show that larger search budgets do not consistently improve performance. The results support selective test-time search for action-chunking policies; evaluation on language-conditioned VLAs and physical robots remains future work.

Abstract: Hierarchical Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (H-JEPA) enable long-horizon visual control but rely on costly test-time gradient descent for planning. Our analysis reveals that iterative optimization consumes up to 97.7% of episode runtime and that low predicted rollout costs do not necessarily translate into successful control. We further identify a planning-horizon asymmetry between long-horizon top-level composition and short two-step subordinate planning. To address these challenges, we propose WARP-JEPA, a hierarchical amortized planning framework that replaces online gradient optimization with learned multi-candidate trajectory proposers while keeping the world models frozen. WARP-JEPA combines a lightweight world-model-in-the-loop causal Transformer at the top level with parallel residual MLPs at subordinate levels. The proposers are trained using model-based rollout costs and best-of-K behavior cloning, enabling efficient candidate selection without test-time gradient optimization. Across 30 frozen H-JEPA models on four benchmarks, WARP-JEPA achieves 9.6-18.8x faster episode inference on long-horizon tasks (2.9-3.0x on Cube) and improves task success by up to 33.3 percentage points. These results demonstrate that amortized planning can substantially improve both inference efficiency and control performance without retraining the world models. Abstract: Conflicting stakeholder objectives make it difficult to coordinate investments in urban resilience. We model conflict-driven urban design as a non-zero-sum digital board game in which stakeholder agents allocate scarce resources and balance individual payoffs against shared urban development and resilience objectives. We use multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) to train policies and examine their behavior across three settings. On a 4 x 4 board, learned policies reach about 84% of the total return found by a simulated annealing benchmark. On an 8 x 8 board with spatial and resource constraints, trained agents favor projects that improve resilience in self-play. In a 12 x 12 simulation initialized from historical maps of Tatsumi, Tokyo, trained agents achieve a mean total score of 452.1, compared with 357.3 for a random-policy baseline across 10,000 simulation runs. Gains in adaptability, sustainability, flood resistance, and greenery come with reductions in vitality and density. A human-in-the-loop interface supports replay and lets participants take over stakeholder decisions, providing a testbed for exploring these trade-offs in participatory urban planning.

Abstract: Tree decision diagrams (TDDs), recently introduced by Capelli et al. (SAT 2026), are representations of Boolean functions. They generalize OBDDs by replacing variable orders with vtrees. The width of a TDD counts the distinct subfunctions across each vtree cut. Counting subfunctions is blind to linear structure. We introduce algebraic tree decision diagrams (ATDDs) by replacing this count with the rank of the cut matrix. The maximum of these ranks over all cuts, the factor rank, is exactly the width of the ATDD. Every TDD can be converted into an ATDD of no greater width. An ATDD is a tree tensor network in canonical form. ATDDs are exponentially more succinct than TDDs, and quasi-polynomially more succinct than structured deterministic DNNFs. Our main result is that this gain in succinctness does not cost canonicity. ATDDs are the first strict canonical form for a rank-based representation over arbitrary vtrees. For each function and vtree there is exactly one ATDD, over the rationals and without any genericity assumption. The canonicity admits a local characterization. Whether a tree tensor network is the ATDD of the Boolean function it computes can be checked in polynomial time from its tensors alone. Equivalence checking becomes a test of syntactic equality. The operation set includes polynomial-time model counting, weighted model counting, and a test whether a tree tensor network represents a Boolean function.
  Capelli et al. asked for the complexity of changing the vtree of a TDD and suggested local vtree changes. We answer the question. After a rotation of the vtree at one node, the new ATDD differs from the old one in two tensors only. Vtree change runs in output-polynomial time both for TDDs and for ATDDs.
  We formalize and verify our core results in Lean. We provide a prototype implementation of TDD and ATDD compilers as a first empirical validation.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become an important way to improve how language models reason, solve problems, and use tools. But getting it to work in practice is not always straightforward. Training can fail because a model receives no useful reward, learns to exploit the reward system, stops exploring, or appears to improve when the evaluation itself is misleading.
  In this book, we give a practical introduction to reinforcement learning post-training for language models, focusing on what works, what fails, and why. We begin with the foundations of post-training, explain GRPO and the training loop step by step, and then follow five training programs covering math reasoning, search agents, training stability, mixture-of-experts models, and software engineering tasks. Each program shows the problems encountered, the changes made, and the lessons learned.
  The book also examines what reinforcement learning actually changes inside a model. The remaining chapters provide a practical reference for reward design, evaluation, training tool-using agents, debugging common failures, reproducibility, rollout engines, and hardware. Written for researchers and practitioners working with open models, the book aims to help readers understand how to train models, recognize when something goes wrong, and decide what to try next.

Abstract: Running a language model involves more than its trained weights. The same model can receive very different benchmark scores, produce different answers, or cost more to serve depending on how it is evaluated and run. In one experiment in this book, changing only the maximum response length moved accuracy on a math benchmark from 0.08% to 93.18%.
  This book explains how inference settings shape the results we report and the systems we deploy. We begin with evaluation, including generation limits, answer extraction, scoring methods, and quantization. Abstract: Natural images are often conjectured to lie on a low-dimensional manifold within the high-dimensional pixel space. Recent work leverages this to build self-contained pixel-space diffusion models without specialized machinery or pretrained components. A plain Transformer on raw image patches suffices, provided it predicts the clean image rather than the noise and compresses each patch through an aggressive bottleneck. The mixing operator, however, has remained softmax attention, inherited from sequence modeling rather than derived from denoising. For Gaussian noise, the optimal denoiser admits a canonical expansion in polynomial functionals of the observation, the Wiener--Volterra series. We prove that softmax attention, and any normalized mixing rule (including linear attention), cannot realize any polynomial map beyond an affine one. In contrast, Volterra layers realize this class directly and, on a data manifold, achieve approximation rates governed by the manifold dimension rather than the ambient pixel dimension. We therefore propose the architecture these results prescribe; raw image patches passed through a bottleneck embedding, followed by cascaded low-rank Volterra layers, which carry no activation function because each layer is nonlinear in its own right. To our knowledge this is the first pixel-space diffusion architecture whose mixing operators are Volterra layers throughout, with no attention, no convolution, no pretrained components, and no activation function on the residual path. On ImageNet $256 \times 256$ and $512 \times 512$, the resulting model is competitive with self-contained pixel-space diffusion at matched parameter and FLOP budgets.

</details>

Abstract: Much of the automation handed to language-model agents is procedural: each step needs little intelligence, but a long unattended run must follow a skill or SOP stated once at the start. We show that agents drift from such procedures over long runs, and that restating the instructions or resuming a stopped agent only moves the drift to another shortcut. We study an executable finite-state machine (FSM) that keeps the workflow state outside the model's context and enforces it at three points: tool calls, state transitions and the end of the agent's turn. In a pre-registered experiment on 200-account SOP-Bench batches, Claude Haiku 4.5 with the FSM decided on average 139 accounts correctly and by the procedure, against 48 for the strongest baseline (95% CI of the difference [46, 130]), and kept this rate to the end; across three models, the gain tracked how much the plain agent drifted. Because the runtime, not the model, supplies this long-horizon reliability, the FSM can let small models carry procedural work: Haiku 4.5 with it averaged more such decisions than plain Claude Sonnet 4.6 at 38% of the cost. Analyses on SWE-bench Verified, $τ^2$-bench, and staged and cross-context workflows further suggest that reliable long-horizon automation can come from stronger execution control, not only stronger models.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit approximate recall: they retain coarse associations about past events while losing fidelity on specific details. Past research on LLMs has quantified different models' forecasting ability in real-world events using prediction markets, but has not studied how losing said fidelity impacts their forecasting abilities. For example: while an LLM correctly recalls that the answer to: ''Did Anna Sawai win the Best Drama Actress in the 76th Emmy Awards?'' is ''Yes,'' it incorrectly states the event date as September 22 rather than September 15. In this paper, we introduce a novel metric called the Recall Fidelity Score to study how imprecision in internalized knowledge propagates to forecasting capability on future events. On resolved historical markets, we find that higher local recall fidelity around semantically similar past events predicts lower forecasting error on resolved markets, and the same signal predicts smaller distance from open markets prices.

</details>

Abstract: Human-AI complementarity captures the idea that a human assisted by an AI system can outperform both the human and the AI acting alone. In prediction tasks, this improvement is assessed against ground truth labels produced through a reference construction procedure and intended to represent the state of affairs to be predicted. Many human-AI interactions, including those where medical AI provides treatment recommendations, however, concern tasks other than predicting what obtains. Their relevant output concerns adequacy relative to a practical goal and depends on several evaluative dimensions. In this work, we examine the ground truth dependence of complementarity and extend it to human-AI interactions where ground truth is not the appropriate reference. We show that tasks beyond prediction can still admit a reference construction procedure that generates adequacy labels. We develop a route for extending complementarity to such settings. It replaces ground truth labels with context-dependent adequacy labels generated through a two-step scoring methodology inspired by multi-criteria decision analysis frameworks in healthcare and it makes the normative choices underlying reference construction explicit. Because the construction of adequacy labels embeds evaluative choices, we draw methodological inspiration from computational reliabilism to identify technical, scientific, and social factors relevant to supporting their construction. Our approach expands the empirical study of human-AI complementarity beyond prediction tasks, enabling its quantitative evaluation in decision-support settings where ground truth is not the appropriate evaluative reference.

</details>

Abstract: Detectors of AI-generated text are usually tuned and tested on passages of several hundred tokens, yet many real cases are short. We study score-based detection as a function of passage length. We give a closed form for how the false-positive rate of a threshold set on long text changes as length falls, and a formula for the number of tokens a detector needs to reach a target true-positive rate. We calibrate thresholds within length bins with conformal p-values, and we add a bin-level rule that abstains when the detector cannot be informative at that length. On synthetic scores the formulas agree with simulation, and binned calibration holds the false-positive rate at its nominal value at every length. A single threshold set at 200 tokens gives 29% for Binoculars at 10 tokens and about 1% for log-likelihood, as the closed form predicts. No detector reaches an 80% true-positive rate, and the best, Binoculars at 200 tokens, reaches 59%, so the rule abstains in every cell at that floor and releases only the 200-token bins at floors of 0.3 and 0.5.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly capable of discovering new mathematical results, yet their reasoning and generated proofs can be difficult to understand and verify. We introduce an interpretable framework for AI-guided mathematical discovery, focused on improving quantitative bounds on analytic constants. Our key insight is that existing proofs often contain slack in intermediate lemmas, which can be tightened without reconstructing the entire argument. To exploit this structure, we represent proofs as partially-formalized Lean blueprints: human-interpretable dependency graphs of definitions, lemmas, and theorems. An AI agent then iteratively refines the Lean code while preserving the blueprint's dependency structure, allowing local improvements to automatically propagate to a verified final bound. This interpretable structure allows humans to inspect the agent's refinements and understand how local improvements propagate to the final theorem. For Grothendieck's constant ($\mathcal{K}_G$), we obtain $$
  \mathcal{K}_G \leq
  \fracπ{2\log(1+\sqrt{2})} - 10^{-17}, $$ an explicit numerical improvement over Krivine's 1977 bound. For Korenblum's constant ($\mathcal{K}_K$), we obtain $$
  \mathcal{K}_K \geq 0.356565, $$ improving Wang's 2025 bound of $0.3554$. Our experiments demonstrate the potential of both partial formalization and iterative agentic refinement for interpretable mathematical discovery.

Abstract: Graph-based retrieval-augmented generation (GraphRAG) improves multi-hop question answering, but deployed systems face two costs that research comparisons rarely measure: building the graph, and removing documents from it. We present Lightfoot, a GraphRAG system in which every entity, link, fact and synonym edge is a deterministic function of per-passage extractions carrying chunk-level provenance. Indexing makes one language-model call per passage, retrieval makes none, and deleting a document is a database operation with no model calls. On the full 11,402-passage MuSiQue corpus we verify that, after deleting 10% of the documents, the graph is identical to a fresh index of the surviving documents. We compare Lightfoot with HippoRAG 2, LightRAG and fast-graphrag under identical extraction, answer and embedding models on MuSiQue, 2WikiMultiHopQA and HotpotQA. Lightfoot builds the cheapest index on all three datasets, 26-31% below HippoRAG 2 and 3-6x below LightRAG and fast-graphrag. Against HippoRAG 2 it is significantly better on MuSiQue retrieval (+2.8 points recall@5 and +4.3 recall@10 on a held-out split) and on HotpotQA answers (+7.7 points exact match), and significantly worse on 2WikiMultiHopQA (-3.9 recall@2, -8.4 F1). Abstract: Method preconditions specify when a method may be applied in hierarchical task network (HTN) planning, but their meaning differs under two established semantics. The SHOP planners, like the classical HTN formalism of Erol, Hendler and Nau, require the precondition to hold immediately before the method's first action. HDDL, the input language of the International Planning Competition's HTN track, instead introduces an auxiliary action to check it; under partial order, unrelated actions may invalidate the precondition before the method starts. HDDL's designers adopted this weaker semantics because the first-action reading could not easily be compiled. Subsequent work has repeatedly identified its compilation under partial order as an open question, with existing transformations limited to total order. We give a compilation of the first-action semantics into standard partially ordered HTN planning without method preconditions using only two additional facts, thereby resolving a longstanding question in the HTN planning community.

</details>

Abstract: Long-context applications, long-lived agents, and multi-agent systems require large language models (LLMs) to process and retain extensive information. In these settings, the KV cache can exceed GPU high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity, necessitating offloading to host memory, where CPU-to-GPU transfers can dominate inference latency. While KV cache compression can reduce data movement, query-agnostic compression that guarantees accurate attention for arbitrary queries is extremely challenging, if not impossible. We introduce VerKV, a hybrid approach that provides $(ε, δ)$ guarantees for approximating attention on any given query. VerKV maintains a compact set of query-agnostic pivotal tokens on the GPU, together with a token-level sketch of the full KV cache, while retaining the complete cache in CPU memory. We introduce a novel stochastic sampling procedure, PoissonComp, to select the pivotal tokens. At inference time, our proposed KVFetch algorithm uses the pivotal tokens and the sketch to estimate the relative error of the resulting attention computation. If the estimate satisfies a user-specified $(ε, δ)$ tolerance, inference proceeds without accessing host memory. Otherwise, VerKV performs query-aware retrieval to fetch additional tokens from the CPU, refining the attention computation to meet the required tolerance. Thus, VerKV enables attention with bounded approximation error while minimizing CPU-to-GPU transfers. We show that PoissonComp outperforms state-of-the-art approaches to KV cache compression. Combined with KVFetch, it achieves near-dense performance (90-100% of dense quality at 32K context length, averaged over RULER and LOFT), using Qwen3-4B, Qwen3.5-9B and Ministral-3-8B, while reducing the GPU memory footprint by 8-32x and keeping CPU-to-GPU memory fetches within 2-23% of the cache per generated token, depending on the model.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning but still produce incorrect solutions to complex problems, indicating the need for more reliable self-correction mechanisms. Mathematical self-correction typically identifies errors through verification, which can proceed backward or forward. The two directions provide complementary verification capabilities. Backward verification checks a candidate answer against the original constraints, bypassing complex intermediate reasoning, but cannot ensure correct reasoning or objective completeness. Forward verification follows the problem conditions and current solution, enabling it to locate reasoning errors and assess objective completeness, but may overlook key constraints in complex solution chains. We propose BiVerify, a dynamically scheduled framework for bidirectional mathematical verification. BiVerify begins with backward verification to screen the answer against the original constraints, followed by forward verification of the reasoning process; subsequent direction switching, state resetting, and re-verification are governed by verification outcomes. Upon failure, the system revises the candidate or verification flow until the same candidate passes both directions. We also introduce shared mathematical tools that separate logical reasoning from precise computation. We systematically evaluate BiVerify on five challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks using multiple mainstream DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini models. BiVerify exceeds the strongest representative public baseline evaluated in this work by 13.5 percentage points in average accuracy, with the margin reaching 31.2 percentage points on GPT. Further analysis shows that BiVerify's overall performance exceeds that of either verification direction alone.

</details>

Abstract: Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.

We introduce DriveFoundry, a closed-loop framework improving iteration efficiency under data and computation budgets by coupling structured causal data refinement with adaptive inference. The Data Engine builds traceable scene-candidate records linking scene evidence and factor-decision relations to candidate trajectories and outcome assessments. Candidate comparisons and counterfactual annotations clarify how scene factors affect behavioral feasibility and preference. Verified model failures guide VLM-assisted annotation and expert correction, refining training data and annotation systems. The model engine, Xiaomi-Drive-o1, unifies Perception, Causation, Action, World, and Value within a shared vision-language backbone to enable cross-task knowledge transfer. A learned value-of-computation head selects tasks and allocates latent tokens based on expected benefit and cost, adapting inference to each decision. On PhysicalAI, the 9B model achieves the lowest ADE@1 across evaluated splits and horizons among tested models. We present InfraRSI, the agentic harness that turns open-ended human commands into verified training optimizations via a five-stage workflow: init locks editable scope and a fixed task contract over workload, loss, and timing invariants to prevent reward hacking, doctor profiles baseline phases and calibrates cluster noise, run co-optimizes launch knobs and code patches before re-verifying top candidates against the baseline and logging findings to a cross-hardware experience bank, and report and apply audit source paths, track failed-trial costs, and commit promoted changes. Rather than stepping greedily from a single incumbent, InfraRSI initializes from diverse experience-seeded configurations and fits global execution-time and censored peak-memory models across all trials, including OOMs. Using these models, it prices per-setting memory costs, revises miscalibrated LLM priors from live runs, and pairs memory-hungry optimizations with memory savers and verified code patches. Across four single- and multi-node dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLM workloads, InfraRSI achieves a speedup of up to $2.75\times$ and lifts mean Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) from $17.4\%$ to $30.0\%$, outperforming existing tuners and coding agents while transferring experience across GPUs. As it improves training infrastructure efficiency, InfraRSI forms a two-level Recursive Self-Improvement loop---revising priors online within runs and distilling experience across clusters---to push foundation model scaling.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents acting across web applications and online services often need multi-turn corrections to align their work with users' underspecified goals and preferences. In practice, users may identify problems in the current outcome without specifying the desired result, so learning user intentions requires interpreting each correction against the agent's preceding and revised outcomes. These expectations are often connected to a particular context, making it hard to know when they apply and how to ensure the agent later acts as the user expects. To this end, we propose LiveAsset, an intent harness that learns reusable intent assets from corrective interactions and uses them for runtime control of agent execution. LiveAsset jointly interprets each correction with the agent's original and revised results to infer user intent, incrementally updating the assets while preserving earlier requirements that remain applicable. The learned requirements guide agent execution from task entry and provide criteria for runtime verification, which uses the explanations to assess applicability before each reply is delivered and directs the agent to revise the result to address unmet requirements while preserving those already satisfied. Following a learning phase in which simulated users progressively reveal requirements through multi-turn corrections, we evaluate LiveAsset using same-task replay of the original requests on π-Bench, PERMA, and Vita and cross-task transfer on PAST-Bench and SkillEvolBench. Experiments show that LiveAsset better fulfills requirements learned from corrective interactions and improves performance on these benchmarks compared with memory, skill, and rule-based harness baselines. Code is available at github.com/landian60/LiveAsset.

Abstract: A tool-using language model must decide whether to retry a failed call, correct it, or stop and tell the user. The error text alone may not be enough: the same feedback can lead to different actions depending on where it appears in the conversation. We isolate this delivery problem by varying placement, guidance, retained history and turn structure. Two studies with plans fixed before data collection, across 13 open models from 3B to 70B parameters, compare the next action and subsequent task completion, with exact deterministic reruns. A new user turn that retains the failed exchange avoids the severe stopping failure observed in Llama models when that exchange is removed. It is non-inferior to history rewriting in pooled synthetic comparisons, but is not best for every model. Given an environment modeled as a Markov decision process and a requirement in probabilistic computation tree logic (PCTL), an LLM writes all weights of a policy network, optionally repairs them on a dataset of optimal actions, and receives model-checking feedback until the requirement holds or a round limit is reached. Abstract: Test-time scaling improves the accuracy of a language model's answers by spending more computation at inference time. Established methods do so either sequentially, revising a single solution over several passes, or in parallel, generating several independent solutions and selecting among them. We present ODSA (Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation), an LLM-directed scaling framework in which an orchestrator LLM is given existing test-time scaling methods as actions --- discovery of a new independent solution, comparison of existing solutions to build a better one, and acceptance of a proposed answer, which triggers its verification --- and directs stateless workers to carry them out. The orchestrator does not solve the problem itself: it allocates computation, feeds the results of earlier actions into later ones, and takes every action from a single context, so that each decision is informed by everything tried before. On Humanity's Last Exam, ODSA improves accuracy by 4.7 to 18.0 points across three models over a single direct call to the model with no test-time scaling. On LiveCodeBench, where a single direct call already solves most problems, it improves accuracy by 0.6 to 14.0 points.

Abstract: The use of generative AI in political campaigns has drawn public concern and policy attention. Public concerns range from distrust of candidates to skepticism about the authenticity and accuracy of political advertising, including AI-generated images and video. Nearly half of U.S. states have enacted laws that prohibit, or require disclosure of, "materially deceptive" AI-generated content (AIGC) in elections to guard against these concerns. This study measures how candidates and their campaigns produce and circulate AIGC during an election cycle. We develop two measures that estimate not only wether campaigns use AIGC, but how much they produce (magnitude) and in what forms (modality). We apply these measures to a novel dataset of text and images from the campaign websites, X accounts, and emails of 12,977 candidates for U.S. political office from 2023 to 2025. We report three findings. First, detectable AIGC from campaigns increased significantly across modalities over the observation window, and campaigns that use AIGC frequently do not disclose it. Second, incumbents' campaigns produced significantly less AIGC than challengers' campaigns. Third, candidates in states with AIGC disclosure or prohibition laws do not produce measurably less AIGC than candidates in other states. Our method and findings have implications for transparency and trust in elections, and on state laws that regulate AI use in campaigns. Because current state laws largely target mainly "materially deceptive" content, we identify additional policy mechanisms that could increase electoral transparency and improve trust as AIGC becomes more prevalent.

Abstract: A deployed language model does not merely answer questions. It spends computation, consumes time, consults evidence, exposes memory, invites or avoids human review, and sometimes makes mistakes whose consequences are radically unequal. Yet LLM systems are still commonly compared as if the relevant object were a single model and the relevant outcome were a single benchmark score. This paper argues that the unit of analysis should instead be the deployment policy: the mechanism that decides when to retrieve, when to remember, when to call a larger model, when to verify, and when not to answer at all. We introduce risk-adjusted deployment efficiency (RADE), a system-level objective that scores the utility a deployment delivers net of inference, retry, oversight, and priced expected failure cost, while treating harms that cannot be priced as hard budgets alongside constraints on latency, privacy, and resources. The framework brings selective routing, retrieval and governed memory, efficient specialization, uncertainty estimation, abstention, and human escalation into a common account. We then examine five deployment regimes in which the same nominal LLM capability leads to different rational architectures, and propose a reporting protocol based on risk-coverage behavior, end-to-end cost, update drift, memory exposure, evidence quality, and escalation load. The central claim is modest but consequential: frontier capability should be treated as a resource to allocate, not a default state to maintain. A safe and sustainable system is not the one that always uses the strongest model; it is the one that spends capability where the reduction in risk or increase in utility justifies the cost.

</details>

Abstract: Continual instruction tuning often preserves old skills with task-private modules. Inference still requires selecting the right module from the prompt alone. We introduce Task-Isolated Latent Adapter Continual Training (TILA-CT), a replay-free method that makes each task module self-indexing. Abstract: Many major scientific advances come from approaching a problem with a new or novel perspective. While large language models (LLMs) with internet-scale knowledge seem poised to take such perspectives, their consistent generation remains elusive. Instead, models are drawn to dominant views and preexisting, in-field bias. To explore the potential of fresh eyes to increase creativity, this paper proposes naive LLMs, models that are made intentionally ignorant of a specific topic. Through deprivation in training or intentional forgetting, naive models are unburdened by existing dogma, allowing them to be free to make new associations and conjectures. These naive models can then be paired with a stronger mentor model that can refine their unique perspectives into concrete proposals. For the 1B and 7B models tested in this paper, naive models consistently produce seed ideas that are more novel and useful than baseline counterparts, and mentor refinement improves the ideas even further. Thus, contrary to the prevailing tradition of scaling up training data, we demonstrate a case where less information is actually more powerful, in particular when the aim is to discover genuinely outside-the-box solutions.

Abstract: The Pravda Network is a Russian state-aligned media aggregator, with over 110 websites and more than 15 million articles published since 2023. Given its prolific and repetitive output, emerging scholarship argues that the Pravda Network was built to influence AI systems. We conduct a large-scale audit of leading AI chatbots to understand: (1) the retrieval rates of Pravda Network articles, (2) the conditions of retrieval, and (3) whether chatbot responses affirm or caveat this content. We build a 10,000 prompt corpus from sampled Pravda Network headlines varied along two dimensions (headline rewording and user skepticism) and query 11 AI chatbots via the web interface (n=104,997 completed responses). Overall, we find susceptibility to Pravda Network retrieval to be infrequent---appearing in 7.3% of responses---and highly platform dependent---with three chatbots responsible for 94% of retrievals (You.com, Claude, and Copilot). Among the frontier models, Claude is an outlier: ChatGPT, Gemini, and Meta AI retrieved no Pravda Network articles, likely indicative of a domain-level filtering policy. All 11 chatbots, however, retrieved other Russian state-aligned media to some degree (median=13.56% of responses). When Pravda Network articles are surfaced, caveat rates vary widely by platform (from 5.3% to 76.2%), with You.com providing the fewest caveats. We also identify the tactic of headline washing: where 9.92% of sampled Pravda Network headlines attribute their reporting to other, often more credible, media outlets, while the articles themselves are republished from Russian state-aligned media. We discuss headline washing's potential to launder state media as independent reporting. With this audit, we provide a systematic analysis of AI chatbots' susceptibility to Russia's latest attempt to manipulate the information environment.

</details>

Abstract: Real-world intelligent systems must reconcile two demands: the adaptability conferred by emergence and the steerability required for accountable deployment. We show that these demands are governed by two distinct bifurcations on the same object and that, consequently, increased capability does not imply increased controllability. Emergence is a bifurcation of the capability variable $S$, with criterion $Γ:=\mathcal F'(0)>1$ equivalent to the existence of a unique positive stable fixed point of a self-consistency equation, following solely from the concavity of $\mathcal F$. Controllability is a bifurcation of the permission variable $c$, whose dynamics include an erosion term and exhibit a saddle-node fold with hysteresis as the gate criterion $ρ^\ast$ crosses unity. Three quantitative results follow: the critical exponent distinguishes families rather than classifying the transition; the gating-cost bound $K(M)\propto\log(1+M)$ grows with scope, not capability; and the fold threshold is $γ_c\approx4μκ$. Calibrated in both directions, the criteria reproduce the two-dimensional Ising transition, admit two true-feedback order parameters, and reject nine candidate quantities, supporting the conclusion that the order parameter is geometric rather than capability-related. Since permission cannot be endogenized and no deployed system has been measured to satisfy $Γ>1$ and $ρ^\ast>1$ simultaneously, increased capability does not imply controllability.

</details>

Abstract: Many of the most consequential advances promised by AI, from curing diseases and inventing new technologies to formulating new scientific theories, are fundamentally creative acts. Yet creativity is uniquely difficult to learn because it faces a creative paradox: learning to repeat past examples of creative success makes those specific examples more likely to recur, inhibiting the very creativity desired. This work argues that creativity is better modeled as an explicit process rather than as a property of individual artifacts, and proposes to learn the underlying generative principles behind the creative process. In particular, to escape the paradox, we introduce creative priming (CP), wherein a trained priming model generates a prompt-specific primer that assists a response model in being creative, explicitly decoupling the creative process from response generation. The priming model learns through GRPO, rewarding primers that lead to responses that are novel, high-quality, and diverse, thereby reinforcing the process that yields creative responses without reinforcing any specific response.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems have demonstrated remarkable effectiveness in mitigating hallucinations by incorporating external knowledge. However, the retrieval process inevitably introduces noise, posing significant challenges to RAG robustness. Fundamentally, noise robustness is a composite competency encompassing noise identification, retrieval filtering, and robust reasoning. Unfortunately, existing methods often fail to capture this holistic nature, typically restricted to single-dimensional enhancements or hampered by noise-induced optimization interference. To address these limitations, we decompose RAG robustness into three sub-tasks: knowledge boundary perception, retrieval verification, and robust reasoning. Accordingly, we propose ARMOR, an Adaptive Curriculum Meta-Learning for Noise Robust RAG Reasoning. Inspired by the teaching process, ARMOR introduces an adaptive task scheduler as a teacher to curate training plans according to the absolute learning progress of RAG generators (Student). Distinct from GRPO, ARMOR utilizes inter-group advantage estimation during learning to effectively mitigate mode collapse caused by limited output spaces. Experiments on three complex reasoning datasets (2WikiMultiHopQA, MusiQue, and HotPotQA) indicate that ARMOR achieves synergistic improvements in retrieval noise robustness, significantly outperforming baselines in output quality.

Abstract: Distributed artificial intelligence (AI) increasingly relies on wireless networks, where communication and computing resources shape how AI tasks are configured and executed. However, resource availability alone does not determine how effectively these tasks can be supported, since different information selections and transmission strategies may yield different AI performance under the same resource constraints. To address this challenge, this article presents Wireless AI Networks as a unified design perspective for bidirectional adaptation between wireless communication and distributed intelligence. Specifically, an AI-executable resource budget characterizes candidate AI configurations, while task-level information value guides their selection and wireless resource allocation. Building on these principles, a wireless-AI co-adaptation framework coordinates resource abstraction, joint configuration and allocation, and execution feedback. Two complementary experiments illustrate this perspective: update selection and statistical correction improve federated learning under identical model-update payload budgets, while sensitivity-aware power allocation reduces model accuracy degradation under the same average transmit-power constraint. Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models have emerged as promising paradigms for decoding brain activity through large-scale self-supervised pretraining. Notably, most existing approaches employ masked patch reconstruction, in which randomly selected EEG patches are masked and recovered by the network. However, purely random masking may limit representation learning as informative patches from highly correlated channels often remain unmasked, causing the network to underutilize weakly correlated channels or serial dependencies. Furthermore, because these models are typically based on transformer architectures, their performance is influenced by the choice of positional encoding. Although positional encoding has been extensively explored in natural language processing and computer vision, few studies have adapted it to the unique characteristics of EEG signals. To address these limitations, this study introduces a conditional positional encoding method based on graph convolutional networks (GCNs) to explicitly model inter-channel correlations, along with an adaptive masking strategy that enhances representation learning by modulating the difficulty of masked patch reconstruction. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to integrate graph-based representations into conditional positional encoding and introduce adaptive masking within EEG foundation model pretraining. Experimental results on ten benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed GCN-based positional encoding and adaptive masking strategies, both individually and in combination, achieve state-of-the-art or competitive performance, highlighting their effectiveness in EEG foundation modeling.

Abstract: We study how to combine a human binary call (a referee's offside or onside decision) with an AI numerical measurement (VAR's estimate of an attacker's position relative to the offside line). We derive an explicit maximum-likelihood rule that determines when the referee's call should stand and when a conflicting VAR measurement should overturn it. Our model treats the true offside margin as fixed and unknown and assumes independent additive Gaussian errors in the human's perception and the AI measurement. When both errors have zero means and known standard deviations $σ_H$ and $σ_{AI}$, respectively, the reversal threshold is $\sqrt{2/π}\,σ^2_{AI}/σ_H$. An onside call is overturned precisely when VAR's measured offside margin exceeds this threshold---not merely because VAR places the attacker beyond the offside line. We also derive the rule when either source has a systematic bias. We prove that the maximum-likelihood estimate of the true margin exists and is unique, while the final binary decision requires only a threshold comparison, without computing that estimate numerically. We illustrate the rule through numerical examples and show how to estimate the error parameters from calibration data. The same analysis applies beyond soccer to binary classification problems in which a human call and an AI measurement satisfy the stated model. This paper is intended for educational purposes: we aim to illustrate how undergraduate students and advanced high school students familiar with basic probability and calculus can investigate research questions in AI and its applications.

Abstract: Out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization remains a central challenge in materials property prediction, where models must extrapolate to novel chemistries beyond the training distribution. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong scientific reasoning, their direct use for quantitative prediction remains limited. To address this, we systematically evaluate multiple roles for LLM assistance, finding that only taxonomy-based grouping consistently improves OOD generalization over a strong kNN+GBT baseline. Building on this, we here propose MOTIF, a framework that leverages LLMs not as predictors, but as generators of candidate taxonomies that partition materials into chemically meaningful environments. These taxonomies are evaluated via training-only pseudo-OOD validation, and only those that improve generalization are retained. Invariant feature selection is then applied within each accepted taxonomy, followed by gated aggregation across taxonomies. We evaluate on 2D halide perovskites, where bandgap prediction must generalize across distinct organic spacer families. On this benchmark, MOTIF reduces mean OOD MAE from 0.122 to 0.111 eV. Under a frozen dev/test protocol, the deployable configuration further improves errors from 0.123 to 0.117 eV (electronic bandgap) and from 0.181 to 0.173 eV (optical bandgap). Beyond this primary task, we validate transferability across additional materials datasets, including 3D perovskite solar cell efficiencies and Matbench benchmarks with structure-based splits. Across domains, MOTIF consistently improves robustness under distribution shift, outperforming expert-defined groupings, rule-based heuristics, and clustering baselines, while remaining competitive with strong classical selection methods. These results demonstrate that LLMs are most effective not as predictors, but as tools for discovering structure in data.

Abstract: Large language models have improved Verilog generation, yet functional correctness remains difficult to optimize because simulation commonly provides a coarse module-level pass/fail outcome. Such feedback discards useful evidence from partially correct RTL modules and cannot determine whether a source statement is functionally responsible for an observed output behavior. We present a new method named Causal Signal Credit Assignment (CSCA), which is a counterfactual preference-optimization framework for Verilog generation. CSCA first performs continuous signal-level behavioral verification on sampled candidate modules. It then locates output-driving assignment spans and applies five interventions while holding the remaining module and testbench fixed. The resulting changes in signal-level errors are combined with behavioral agreement to form a continuous statement credit, which is mapped to response-token weights for strict weighted direct preference optimization. This enables CSCA to learn from locally supported statements without uniformly reinforcing an otherwise incorrect response. Built on Icarus Verilog and a CodeV-derived corpus, CSCA is evaluated on VerilogEval~2.0 and RTLLM~v2.0, including both code completion and specification-to-RTL tasks. With a Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct backbone, CSCA leads the evaluated 7B-scale models and remains comparable to the much larger GPT-5.4-mini on the reported Verilog-generation benchmarks.

Abstract: Distribution Matching Distillation~(DMD) enables strong few-step diffusion generation by matching the student distribution to a pretrained teacher. Existing improvements to DMD often augment its supervision or modify its optimization machinery. We ask a complementary question: \emph{can DMD diagnose its own updates using signals already produced during training?} DMD inherently computes the discrepancy between a pretrained teacher and a fake model tracking the student distribution, but conventionally uses it primarily to determine the distribution matching direction. We find that the magnitude of this teacher--fake disagreement provides additional diagnostic information. Across extensive experiments, large disagreement in the high-noise regime consistently coincides with substantially higher-energy DMD updates. This reveals a dual role of disagreement: it determines not only \emph{where} the student should move, but also provides an intrinsic signal of \emph{how aggressively} the update should be applied. Based on this observation, we introduce Disagreement-Aware Distribution Matching Distillation (DA-DMD), which activates only in the high-noise regime and continuously modulates the update strength according to teacher--fake disagreement. DA-DMD reuses quantities already available during DMD training and requires no additional model, evaluator, or inference-time modification. We show that it exactly recovers vanilla DMD outside the selected region, reduces update energy only through selected samples, and exhibits redescending influence under extreme disagreement. Across multiple diffusion backbones, DA-DMD consistently improves few-step generation at the same inference cost. On SD3.5-L, DA-DMD improves HPSv2 from $28.23$ to $30.93$, while achieving a $64.4\%$ average win rate over DMD2.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal Emotion Reasoning (MER) requires perceiving and integrating emotion cues from multiple modalities. Recent advances increasingly leverage Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to enhance reasoning capacity. However, due to the inherent heterogeneity in conveying emotions across modalities, these models often suffer from modality imbalance and confront flawed emotion understanding. In this paper, we conduct an in-depth analysis on MER models and uncover two key empirical insights. (i) In MER, modality imbalance manifests two distinct failure modes: unimodal over-reliance and insufficient cross-modal utilization, which call for different intervention strategies. (ii) MLLM layers exhibit implicit functional hierarchies in multimodal emotion processing, playing separable roles in stabilizing unimodal representations and integrating cross-modal information. Motivated by these observations, we propose a Layer-Aware Modality Rebalancing (LAMR) framework by explicitly aligning intervention strategies with layer roles. Specifically, we first introduce two quantized metrics for adaptive layer role profiling. In each layer, Modality Entropy (ME) measures the dispersion of modality contributions, and Cross-modal Intensity (CMI) captures the strength of cross-modal interactions. Then, we respectively enhance modality equilibrium in stabilization layers and cross-modal propagation in integration layers. Extensive experiments on two comprehensive MER benchmarks demonstrate that LAMR effectively mitigates modality imbalance and improves emotion reasoning across diverse MLLM backbones, without additional training.

</details>

Abstract: Wearables, mobile devices, and IoT platforms generate multimodal time-series data continuously and at scale, making efficient storage and transfer a primary concern. The same recordings are reused after collection by many downstream models, often at different temporal resolutions, so a compressed representation should be small, useful across resolutions, and produced at a bit rate known in advance. Prior work meets parts of this: symbolic and error-bounded compressors reconstruct accurately but fix resolution at compression time, learned codecs target one signal domain, and discrete tokenizers reconstruct without accounting for rate. We introduce CHORD, a coarse-to-fine compressor for heterogeneous multimodal time series. A residual cascade orders each payload from broad to fine temporal scales and synthesizes corrections through learned shapelet-inspired atoms. A shared pathway supplies a reusable synthesis basis across modalities, while private pathways retain capacity for sensor-specific morphology. Finite scalar quantization fixes the bit width of every modality and scale, so payload size is known before encoding, and scale dropout trains each coarse-to-fine prefix to be a valid reconstruction operating point. Across seven wearable-sensing and forecasting benchmarks, CHORD stays within 0.62 points of uncompressed classification accuracy on average at the 8x target, the smallest gap of any compressed method, with all resolutions decoded from one payload.

</details>

Abstract: In most LLM agents, weights are fixed after deployment, so new information must be retained in external memory. Existing approaches often rely on predefined records and fixed organizational rules, which cannot readily accommodate evolving tasks and interactions. This raises the question: can LLM agents organize their own memory using ordinary documents? We introduce self-organized memory (SoMem), a framework that stores memory in linked documents. The agent decides what to summarize, how to group related information, and when to revise existing topics, and every summary it produces stays linked to the original interactions as evidence. The runtime validates updates and automatically derives temporal, relational, and recent-memory views from the organized content. At query time, the agent chooses what to search and read, following document links and inspecting original evidence as needed. On LoCoMo, BEAM-100K, and LongMemEval-S, SoMem outperforms the strongest baseline by 2.66, 3.10, and 2.00 percentage points, respectively. The constructed memory can be reused across models, and additional reasoning effort can further improve answer quality. These findings demonstrate the feasibility of agents organizing and using their own persistent memory through ordinary documents. Code is available at: https://github.com/Qi202/SoMem.

Abstract: Evidence-sufficiency benchmarks ask whether a model can tell a complete passage set from one whose reasoning chain is broken. We present ChainCheck, an evaluation-validity audit that pairs validator-certified counterfactual cells with artifact-only baselines. In an exploratory crossed control on 2,214 HotpotQA pairs, a language-model scorer that never reads the chain reaches a nominal AUC of 0.997, and a one-string match reaches the maximum of the crossed chain-sensitivity statistic CS4 (0.500), while the 11 evaluated systems score from 0.308 to 0.484; the two statistics are different metrics. We therefore read positive CS4 as operational bridge-consistency sensitivity, not as semantic multi-hop reasoning. Every system is also sensitive to one extra edit, and the edits are detectable according to model-based naturalness ratings. LLM-driven search can automate strategy design through repeated proposal and evaluation. This process must identify useful strategies within a limited evaluation budget. The key challenge is that \textit{actual quantization is expensive, while inexpensive proxies may not reflect quantized-model performance.} To address this challenge, we present AutoQS, an \textbf{Auto}mated \textbf{Q}uantization \textbf{S}trategy Design with LLMs. AutoQS keeps weight precision fixed and searches group-size allocations across layers and operators under deployment constraints. Evaluation history guides coordinated LLM proposals, while deterministic validation ensures compatible allocations. An inexpensive quality proxy evaluates candidates, and an candidate repository retains alternative allocations to inform subsequent proposals. Periodic actual quantization complements these proxy evaluations with measured quality and checkpoint storage, providing feedback for further search within the evaluation budget. Experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that AutoQS improves seven-task average accuracy over uniform-group GPTQ and AWQ by 0.70 and 1.12 percentage points, respectively, at four-bit weight precision.

Abstract: In this work, we study whether frontier agent can continually improve real-world AI infrastructure over long horizons. We introduce Infrathon, a benchmark of diverse, long-horizon AI infrastructure optimization tasks across different levels. We evaluate six models across 150 runs with 24-hour budgets, totaling over 10,000 GPU-hours. Our results show that model rankings vary with the evaluation horizon, while 135 of the 150 runs achieve further improvements during the second half of their budgets. These findings indicate that shorter evaluations can overlook both optimization gains and changes in relative model performance. We also find agents can discover solutions that outperform human-designed baselines: Claude Opus 5.5 increases the mean KV-cache compression ratio from 6.82% with the human-designed Quant-VideoGen codec to 19.86%. Analysis of agent trajectories identifies task violations, unreliable testing, and premature completion as barriers to sustained improvement. Abstract: Predicting multi-step chemical reaction mechanisms requires identifying plausible intermediates and correctly ordering the elementary reaction steps. Existing computational methods focus on single-step elementary reaction prediction, limiting their ability to generalize to unseen reaction spaces. Existing benchmarks establish capabilities in domain knowledge, multimodal interpretation and scientific tool use.
