Abstract: Floorplanning is an early step in turning a logical circuit into a physical chip: it positions large circuit modules and constrains the space available for subsequent logic placement and wiring. These decisions affect congestion and timing, but their physical consequences emerge through downstream implementation. For AI agents, the task requires translating engineering constraints into spatial decisions whose quality cannot be judged by successful tool execution alone. We present an evaluation framework that connects submitted layouts to a prescribed downstream implementation flow, checks geometric validity, measures physical quality, and reports resource use. On one industrial design whose 99 macros occupy approximately 71\% of a fixed L-shaped layout region, we compare our specialized system Nova-SI with general-purpose engineering agents and agents restricted to file access, obtaining complete physical measurements for 39 layout outputs. Under our 0--100 composite quality score, Nova-SI achieves a mean of 57.97 without online feedback from the prescribed power-grid and placement evaluation, compared with 31.17 for the strongest measured general-purpose configuration, GPT-6 Astra. The difference is supported by raw congestion and timing measurements and persists across scoring-sensitivity checks. Layout-level analysis further shows that similar global utilization can conceal substantially different local congestion. Together, the protocol and case study assess agents through the physical consequences of their delivered layouts and provide evidence about system performance on this industrial instance.

Abstract: Large language models offer a promising technique for translating natural-language optimization problems into solver code, but their reliability remains constrained by the quality of template retrieval. While recent structured methods improve over flat prompting through hierarchical taxonomy search, their traversal rules remain fixed or LLM-driven at inference time, leaving retrieval unable to improve from past successes and failures. To support extensible retrieval, COMPASS embeds problem descriptions and template nodes in a shared semantic space, allowing newly inserted templates to be scored from their text descriptions without redefining the action space. For the four valid user task descriptions, proper requirements, correct references to the relevant engineering knowledge, correct components and lastly a valid Factory I/O scene are generated. For the task with missing engineering knowledge and a wrong sensor selection, the user is informed about which requirements of the scene generation lack domain knowledge and also which sensor is not compatible with the selected raw material by the user with link to the related documentation. After fixing the task description, the correct scene is seamlessly generated. \textbf{CRAFT-AI} highlights the importance of safeguards for risk perception and communication in foundation model-driven solutions.

Abstract: Biomedical ontologies are structured knowledge systems that must evolve continuously to represent newly emerging knowledge, but their manual maintenance remains time-consuming and laborintensive. Textual definitions are especially costly because they must be readable to humans and compatible with controlled entities, relations, and logical constraints. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising way to assist ontology engineering tasks, including definition generation. However, textual plausibility does not guarantee knowledge-system compatibility. A generated definition may be fluent and close to a reference definition, yet still fail to ground its implied entities or violate ontology-level constraints. Directly inserting such definitions into ontology libraries may therefore introduce entity-level hallucinations and logical inconsistencies. To address this problem, we propose two structured-knowledge incompatibility metrics, Entity Error Rate (EER) and Logical Inconsistency Rate (LIR), to quantify entity grounding and logical consistency for LLM-generated ontology definitions. We further develop an agentic quality-control (QC) workflow that treats definition generation as an iterative process rather than a direct decoding step: it generates definitions, extracts axioms, checks entity existence and logical consistency, and uses the resulting diagnostics to drive repair loops. Comprehensive experiments on Disease Ontology, Uberon Ontology, and Uberon-ConstraintStress demonstrate that EER and LIR complement conventional reference-based metrics by evaluating whether generated content can be grounded and checked inside the target ontology. The workflow effectively decreases EER while maintaining strong conventional text-generation performance. LIR further exposes sparse ontology-level logical failures that are invisible to text similarity metrics, although their repair remains limited.

</details>

Abstract: During reinforcement learning, experience can be both internalized in LLM parameters and retained externally as reusable textual skills. Parameter updates alter the model's behavior and the interaction experience from which new skills are extracted. These skills, in turn, guide the model's subsequent interactions and parameter updates. As the model's capabilities evolve, previously beneficial skills may become redundant or detrimental. We present SkillDuet, a framework that coordinates parameter updates and skill evolution through model response feedback. Skills extracted from on-policy trajectories guide parameter updates through OPD at every step. A response-based score, Strength, ranks candidate skills by the magnitude of the shift they induce in the current model's responses, and the highest-scoring skills are periodically consolidated into a persistent global skill set (Global). The updated model and Global jointly guide subsequent interactions, generating experience for further parameter and skill updates. With Qwen3-4B, SkillDuet achieves success rates of 98.57% on ALFWorld and 92.97% on WebShop, exceeding the matched SEED-API baseline by 2.86 and 5.47 percentage points, respectively. Ablations show that response-based skill selection and Global consolidation improve task performance. Further experiments show that the benefits of retained skills depend on both their content and their compatibility with the model. SkillDuet provides a concrete mechanism for model-skill co-evolution and skill-level recursive self-improvement.

</details>

Abstract: Full-pipeline data analysis over real-world data lakes requires LLM agents to locate and integrate information across large collections of tables with different schemas. Existing data agents follow task-specific exploration, which exhausts the context window in massive lakes. As the context fills, the agent's reasoning degrades, leading it to locate and process incorrect data files. These errors propagate into the final answer. To overcome these limitations, we introduce Ontology-Grounded Data Agent (OGDA), which utilizes a compact ontology to reduce exploration overhead and constrain data processing. OGDA constructs the ontology through agentic hypothesis testing. At each step, a proposer agent induces schemas, concepts, and relations from raw sources, and a verifier checks each proposal against the underlying data. During task execution, the ontology serves as a representation of the data lake for the agent to explore. Every data-processing action is constrained by a task graph, which the agent can extend only after observing the semantic meaning of the data. Across KramaBench and DiscoveryBench with three LLM backbones, OGDA outperforms the strongest baselines by up to 10.82 points. These results suggest that explicitly modeling the data lake is a key step toward reliable end-to-end data agents.

Abstract: Autonomous agents must continually acquire reusable knowledge from experience without relying on costly large language model (LLM) reasoning for every decision. However, existing experience-based approaches often struggle to transform accumulated observations and reasoning into compact knowledge that lightweight models can independently apply. This paper proposes SCALE, a small-model-first framework that integrates selective LLM reasoning, experience-driven concept abstraction, and knowledge internalization. A lightweight model handles routine predictions, while prediction errors and accumulated cross-episode evidence selectively trigger LLM reflection. The LLM analyzes relevant historical experiences, generates positive and negative counterfactual examples, and proposes reusable concept hypotheses. Independently validated concepts are progressively incorporated into hierarchical neural representations, enabling lightweight models to exploit increasingly abstract knowledge. Meanwhile, mastered experiences are excluded from active memory while their original records remain available in a complete archive. Experiments across ten random seeds show that historical recall improves held-out accuracy from 87.11% to 98.28%, hierarchical concept knowledge increases difficult-case accuracy from 55.33% to 66.47%, and abstract archiving reduces active experience records from 6,000 to 1,528.

Nevertheless, there is currently no appropriate way to evaluate such abilities because recent regulatory and methodological shifts, most notably FDA's Project Optimus, have redefined what constitutes a reasonable design and rendered many previous designs obsolete. This reference standard is produced through a formalization of the expert design process. We evaluate five frontier LLMs, none of which matches the reference standard. Our ablation experiments trace this performance gap to two main causes, inference of latent, unrecorded design determinants from trial sources (supplying them raises class-wise balanced accuracy by 0.22) and structured decision-tree reasoning (+0.24). Abstract: Code localizers are usually trained to predict the files a reference patch edits, but the code a fixer needs to read is not the same as the code it changes. We propose Fixer-Guided Localization (FGL): the localizer proposes several localizations, a frozen fixer scores each by how likely it makes a verified patch, and the localizer is trained on these scores with weighted supervised fine-tuning, without running tests and without reinforcement learning. We build the full pipeline on Gemma 4 E4B for the Kaggle Gemma-4 Developer Agent competition, from 1,629 test-verified Kimi K3 trajectories. The FGL target helps the fixer find the bug as often as the teacher's localization while using 27.5% less code. On the development set, one EM round moves the agent in the expected direction compared with a matched control: more tasks reach a patch (53 vs. 46), fewer tasks get stuck reading files that do not exist (43 vs. 54), and more tasks are solved (8 vs. 6). Training also changes how the agent works: trained agents explore about three times more than the base model and write far fewer wrong patches, and the single agent solves up to 11 of 78 tasks, against 8 for the base model. An analysis of 13 evaluation runs identifies what limits a 4B agent under an 8-minute budget (time, repetition loops and edit quality) and gives practical lessons for training small coding agents. All data, code and adapters are publicly available.

Abstract: We introduce FraudBench, an evaluation of large language models' (LLMs) propensities to recommend, assist, or directly take actions that would constitute fraud under U.S. law. Our evaluation contains a novel dataset of 159 scenarios based on real-world U.S. court cases and validated by legal experts. We present each scenario to LLMs via three tasks at varying levels of autonomy: advising users, drafting messages for users to send, and acting directly on behalf of users (477 tasks total). In each scenario, FraudBench re-enacts the facts of a court case to give LLMs an opportunity, but no explicit instruction, to either misrepresent or conceal a material fact in a high-stakes commercial setting--e.g., whether to describe company-funded product testing as "independent" to customers (misrepresentation) or whether to hide unfavorable regulatory investigations from clients (concealment). We evaluate 20 LLMs across 25 experimental conditions (totaling >300,000 generations) and include a preregistered human baseline ($n_{\texttt{annotations}} = 2{,}957$, $n_{\texttt{humans}} = 996$). We score a response as fraudulent when it engages in, assists with, or recommends misrepresentation or concealment. Key findings include: (1) across LLMs, fraud rates ranged from near 0% to over 40%; (2) LLM fraud rates increased with autonomy, with overall rates of 3.0% when advising, 14.7% when drafting, and 21.4% when acting directly, without applying pressure; (3) LLMs sometimes engaged in fraud despite recognizing legal risks; (4) LLMs more often engaged in fraud by concealment than by misrepresentation; and (5) prompt-based interventions substantially affected fraud rates, with a prompt to obey the law reducing fraud rates, while prompts to maximize profits and act agentically increased fraud rates.

</details>

Abstract: A sparse terminal reward tells an agent little about its steps. Much of the missing information is implicit in the environment's dynamics, because each state transition can change the conditions for success long before the reward arrives. A world model trained on real transitions can learn to predict what each action changes, but a predicted observation does not by itself show whether success has become more likely. With a binary terminal reward, PPO's critic makes this judgment by estimating the current policy's probability of success, but only for states the agent reaches. Our method, WM-PPO, elegantly brings the two together. For each action the policy samples, executed or not, the world model predicts the next observation and the critic values the predicted successor. In the policy channel, comparisons among these values give the policy auxiliary advantages; in the value channel, their mean gives the critic an auxiliary target. A shared gate weights both channels according to how reliably comparisons with similar margins predicted real outcomes earlier in training. The policy, critic and world model all learn from the agent's own interaction, with no teacher or privileged information, and only the policy is used at inference. With a comparable number of real environment steps, WM-PPO raises success from PPO's 63.5% to 87.5% on ALFWorld and from 39.3% to 52.9% on ScienceWorld. It matches the strongest PPO refinements we tested on ALFWorld and exceeds the best of them by 6.5 points on ScienceWorld. The trained policy more often completes prerequisite steps before placing an object or committing to an answer.

</details>

We present PARIS (PRISMA Agentic Research Intelligence System), an open-source framework in which a conductor agent running in Claude Code orchestrates 15 specialized large language model (LLM) agents, from scoping a research question to exporting a manuscript draft. Agents are specified declaratively, coordinate exclusively through persisted artifacts that the conductor validates before every transition, and delegate retrieval, bibliometric analysis and scoring to deterministic Python utilities. Three human checkpoints gate study inclusion, corpus adequacy and the synthesis outline. We also introduce QualityScore, a pre-synthesis index that aggregates normalized network, bibliometric, semantic and process sub-scores into a 0-100 grade. Two case studies illustrate both: AI chatbots and university students' learning (388 records, 100 included) and AI-assisted SR methods (131 records, 123 included, QualityScore 69.1 recomputed with a proxy network sub-score). They show end-to-end feasibility across domains and the diagnostic use of QualityScore: in the first run, corpus improvements chosen at the corpus-adequacy checkpoint raised the score from 74.6 to 81.9 and the number of pre-2023 studies from 1 to 19. Audits of both runs exposed failure modes: incomplete retrieval, counts that drifted between agents, unrecorded or delegated checkpoint decisions and a grade that depends on how citation data are handled. Output quality has so far been judged only by an internal reviewer agent; independent validation against human reviews is the next step.

Abstract: Speech comprehension in the human brain is an incremental process that begins with acoustic processing and advances to linguistic processing. While recent studies have revealed that the hierarchy of deep neural networks (DNNs) correlates with the ascending auditory pathway, the exact nature of this DNN-brain alignment remains underexplored. In this study, we investigate how DNN representations align with the brain's acoustic-to-linguistic processing. Specifically, we employed neural encoding models to simulate neural responses to acoustic (i.e., speech and noise envelope) and linguistic features (i.e., word onset and surprisal). By applying representational similarity analysis (RSA), we quantified the similarity between these neural responses and the DNN embeddings generated by a pre-trained automatic speech recognition (ASR) model, both before and after fine-tuning on audiovisual noisy data. Our results demonstrate significant DNN-brain alignment: embeddings from shallow layers exhibit higher similarity to neural responses associated with acoustic features, while those from deeper layers align more closely with neural responses related to linguistic features. Importantly, the fine-tuning process enhances this alignment by improving noise processing in shallower layers and refining linguistic representations in deeper layers. These results suggest that fine-tuned DNN models can naturally develop human-like processing patterns in noisy environments, highlighting a functional alignment between the human brain and DNNs in speech representation.

Abstract: Recurrent latent reasoning in large lanugage models (LLMs) scales test-time computation by increasing recurrent depth, naturally framing inference as a problem of deciding when to stop. We identify a previously overlooked limitation of this single-depth view through systematic preliminary analysis: deeper recurrence can lose correct solutions found at shallower depths, while depth-specific success sets remain broadly non-nested even after controlling for marginal accuracy. This suggests that depth determines not only computation cost, but also access to distinct candidate solutions. Motivated by this finding, we propose Depth Portfolio Allocation (DPA), which reformulates recurrent test-time scaling as budgeted allocation across depth-specific candidate sources. DPA supports both fixed portfolios and feedback-guided sequential allocation under a shared inference budget. Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, MultiArith, and MBPP show that mixed-depth portfolios expand oracle coverage under matched candidate counts and nominal costs, while feedback-guided allocation generally matches or improves uniform allocation and can reduce computation in high-budget settings. Abstract: Action theories typically fix their vocabulary and causal laws, whereas agent harnesses can change their tools, providers, and policies during execution. We introduce Reconfigurable Action Theories (RAT), in which actions can change the action theory itself. A theory module in RAT contains a vocabulary, causal laws, and an initializer. A RAT configuration records the active modules and the world state. Module loading and unloading are represented by actions governed by ordinary causal laws. RAT retains the standard causal action semantics of its language fragment when the active module set is fixed. We give sufficient conditions for at most one next configuration, preservation of existing behavior when a module is added, and equal results when modules are loaded in either order. We establish the computational complexity of reasoning tasks in RAT and translate finite RAT systems into nonmonotonic causal theories and Answer Set Programming (ASP), preserving bounded executions. Experiments compare finite RAT models with executions of official DeepSeek Harness, Pi, and OpenHands implementations. RAT provides a formal basis for checking how component changes affect a harness's available actions, behavior, and reachable goals.

Abstract: Raw radar spectra are widely viewed as too cluttered and noisy for direct representation learning, motivating handcrafted reductions, sparse point clouds, or compact Doppler descriptors in radar perception. We challenge this assumption and argue that the main limitation lies not in the raw representation itself, but in the absence of a learning objective that separates structured signal from noise. Handcrafted reductions partially address this problem but impose rigid assumptions about what constitutes signal, discarding weak yet informative structure while retaining artifacts that fit the reduction model. Once removed, such information cannot be recovered by supervised learning. We propose an alternative: structure is the component of a radar spectrum that remains reconstructable under localized corruption, while noise is the component that does not. This perspective motivates self-supervised masked modeling for dense radar spectra without predefined signal-noise separation. We instantiate it with Leakage-aware Spectral MAE, a masked autoencoder for raw radar cubes using a corruption strategy inspired by cross-bin energy spreading caused by spectral leakage. We also demonstrate the importance of Doppler organization: treating Doppler as a primary spatial dimension in an x-y-D representation, with height encoded as features, substantially outperforms naive channel stacking and enables effective learning from dense 4D spectra. Without handcrafted reductions, our approach consistently outperforms state-of-the-art radar-based detectors and point-cloud-based self-supervised learning (SSL) counterparts. These results establish raw radar spectra as an effective representation for 3D perception when paired with an appropriate pretraining objective.

</details>

Abstract: Speculative decoding speeds up language models by letting a lightweight drafter propose several future tokens that the target model verifies in parallel. Block diffusion drafters such as DFlash draft a whole block in one forward pass, and DDTree builds a draft tree from their per-position distributions. Each of these distributions assumes that the drafter's own most likely continuation is correct. A tree, however, gains over a chain only on branches that leave this continuation, where the drafter's top-1 accuracy drops from 89% to 18% and its candidate lists miss the right token far more often. When re-run from the fork token, the same drafter is usually correct, so the errors come from the prefix that the distributions condition on rather than from what the drafter knows. We introduce DForkTree, a training-free method that re-anchors each branch on its own tokens. DForkTree adds n-gram statistics from the target model's own outputs to the drafter's scores as pointwise mutual information, weights them more after the fork, and builds the optimal tree under these scores exactly on the GPU. On Qwen3.5-4B and 9B, with all tree methods sharing one drafter, tree pipeline and verifier, DForkTree is the fastest tree method on every benchmark. It is up to 41.5% faster than DFlash and 15.1% faster than DDTree in the same setting, with average speedups of up to 4.75x over autoregressive decoding.

</details>

Abstract: Correlation Matrix Memories (CMMs) store associations as sums of rank-one outer products, providing closed-form addressability, exact deletion, and linear attribution. While classical analyses assume random, orthogonal keys, semantic encoders produce correlated representations. We analyse CMMs under correlated keys using frame-theoretic coherence: (i) capacity bounds yielding an encoder-independent limit $n \le d(1+\varepsilon^2)$ for mean squared crosstalk $\varepsilon^2$; (ii) an exact characterisation of the collateral effect of deleting an association, guaranteeing that deletion never invalidates certified associations; and (iii) a geometric transparency bound demonstrating why attribution requires candidate sets of centred keys. On this basis, we introduce Topology-Aware Neuro-Symbolic Associative Retrieval (TANSAR). TANSAR traverses a directed acyclic association graph, prunes invalid paths via sound ground Horn constraints, and injects a representation derived strictly from the verified path into the residual stream of a frozen language model, guarded by a Clopper--Pearson selective-risk gate. Controlled experiments confirm all theoretical bounds. In multi-hop knowledge editing, Horn pruning reduces answer contradictions from 25.1% to 0.5%, whereas Hopfield retrieval incurs 24% contradictions when stale associations remain stored (and 0% if stale associations are removed). An end-to-end edit transaction requires 13.6 ms on a standard CPU and 2.37 ms on GPU. The symbolic component matches gold labels across all 600 ProofWriter theories and achieves 94.0% agreement on FOLIO, demonstrating sound and efficient multi-hop associative retrieval with exact unlearning.

</details>

Abstract: Shadow IT, the use of technology that the IT organisation has not approved, is a long-standing governance problem. We argue that autonomous AI agents can make it grow much faster: agents make building tools nearly free, act with their user's privileges, and leave behind scripts, automations and MCP servers that are themselves unsanctioned. A simple compounding model shows that, under its assumptions, shadow IT grows exponentially once agentic artifacts reproduce faster than the organisation retires them; retiring them starts with detecting them. We then ask whether unsanctioned agents can be detected on Windows endpoints. We propose a taxonomy of nine classes of agents and six layers of endpoint footprint, and argue that rules based only on the structure of processes and connections cannot separate an agent from its behavioural twin, the same program talking to a non-AI service. We present Agent Detector, an open-source Windows detector that anchors behaviour to the identity of the remote service and correlates it with configuration, installation and execution history, using a catalog of 39 tools and 184 LLM endpoint entries; it informs users through a notice they must acknowledge instead of blocking. On a controlled bench of 12 synthetic agents, 3 AI components and 9 benign workloads, repeated 5 times on one workstation, two behavioural rules flagged every behavioural twin, while Agent Detector flagged none and detected every agent in the majority of runs. Its limits are temporal: a connection to a provider behind a shared content-delivery network is attributable only while the DNS cache holds the provider's name, one to five minutes, and an intermittent agent was seen by about one scan in three. A scan costs about 154 ms. The bench is too small to rank detectors; it maps what each kind of evidence can see: unsanctioned agents can be partly detected, and not by behaviour alone.

</details>

Abstract: Micro-scale air quality models require hourly traffic data on every road segment, whereas permanent counters cover only a small part of the network. A graph neural network (GNN) was developed to reconstruct hourly traffic flows on the 32,163 road segments of Paris intra-muros from the 1,573 segments equipped with a counter (4.9%). The model adapts the Graph Neural Network for Urban Interpolation (GNNUI) to a real, sparse counting network. Flows are decoded on the edges of the junction graph (one edge per segment, both directions summed), a traffic climatology serves as an offset in a zero-inflated negative-binomial likelihood, and training proceeds by masking counters. Eight-fold spatial cross-validation, with counters sharing a published series merged before splitting, distinguishes interpolation at a withheld counter in an instrumented district (R${}^2$ = 0.89, error of a quarter of the mean flow) from extrapolation to a district without counters (R${}^2$ = 0.74, a third of the mean flow). These scores hold on main axes where counters are located, while relative errors are about 50% on the few counted local streets. Ablations show that the climatology offset tends to help extrapolation. Road attributes, time cycles and the live state of the counting network matter most, while weather inputs have no significant effect. The masking rate is the only inherited hyperparameter with a clear effect. When evaluated on January--August 2026 (outside the training period), the frozen model stayed around the error band of previous years.

Abstract: As LLM agents become more autonomous, they are increasingly expected to run as always-on assistants on a user's behalf, identifying work to be done and completing it without being asked. Yet existing benchmarks usually give the agent its task, whereas in knowledge work the requests sit in the user's workspace and the agent must first recognise which obligations exist. We study proactive task discovery and execution, in which an agent must infer a user's pending tasks from their workspace and complete them in the systems where the work lives. This setting poses two challenges. For the agent, the tasks are buried in a large volume of mail, chat, documents and records. For evaluation, a benchmark needs a simulated organisation whose people, events and documents are mutually consistent, so that tasks arise realistically and their correct outcomes are known. We introduce AutoBuddy-Bench, a simulator that addresses these challenges. It models an organisation's employees, projects, business systems and event history as a temporal graph, renders it into realistic workspace artefacts, derives ground-truth tasks from the events that create obligations, and computes each task's outcome from the world state; execution is judged by the agent's changes to the workspace. To evaluate current LLMs, we build AutoBuddy, a reference agent with interchangeable backbone LLMs: it identifies tasks from the workspace, grounding each in its source records, and executes each in a separate session with write access. Varying only the backbone, we find a systematic shift in where tasks are lost: weaker models fail mainly at discovery, intermediate models identify tasks but often stop short of carrying them out, and the strongest models fail mainly during execution, most often on business-system operations. Discovery precision is only weakly related to execution quality, suggesting the two are distinct capabilities.

</details>

Abstract: Answer accuracy alone does not distinguish failures in acoustic perception, rule knowledge, and rule application. AuDiR (Audio Diagnostic Reasoning) examines these distinctions through controlled input interventions. Each item contains an audio clip, a multiple-choice question, a task-general rule, and a textual description of the acoustic observations needed to answer. Three conditions progressively supply the rule and observations; a fourth removes the audio while retaining the question and rule. The first correct diagnostic condition assigns an operational response label, without identifying a unique internal cause. The current benchmark contains 201 items in 18 families drawn from 9 sources, with answer references documented separately from percept extraction. In a preliminary evaluation of Gemini-3.8-flash, accuracy is 29.3% without supplied information, 31.3% with the rule, and 98.5% with the observations also supplied. No-audio accuracy is 23.9%, with a confidence interval containing the 25.0% chance rate. A family-level knowledge probe associates incomplete rule recall with larger gains from rule provision. These results show the benefit of supplying acoustic observations as text, while incomplete validation, possible recognition shortcuts, and non-monotonic responses limit stronger failure-attribution claims.

</details>

Abstract: We propose SciMARS, a framework for recursive task-local adaptation of multi-agent harnesses in closed-loop scientific discovery. Multi-agent systems coordinate analysis, experimental design, and verification as scientific evidence evolves. However, two problems limit their effectiveness: (i) without shared memory, agents lose observations and repeat work; (ii) fixed role procedures, handoffs, and memory policies perpetuate mistakes despite retained evidence. We address these problems with a shared evidence graph and separate component editors. SciMARS adapts roles and coordination; SciMARS + Memory adds a third editor for how agents record, retrieve, and receive evidence. Candidate probes guide composition and selection of the next harness. We evaluate scientific discovery through experimental-data replay and a digital twin. SciMARS + Memory resolves 12/18 GPT-6-Astra and 2/18 GLM-5.3 attempts on Autonomous Science Bench, compared with 8/18 and 1/18 for the base multi-agent team. On ScienceAgentBench and AppWorld, it solves 14/38 and 11/20 tasks, compared with 11/38 and 5/20 for the base team. These results support combining shared memory with harness adaptation across scientific and interactive tasks.

Abstract: Urban traffic flow prediction is a cornerstone of intelligent transportation systems, yet accurate forecasting under adverse weather conditions remains a significant challenge. Existing data-driven models, while effective for routine patterns, often struggle with the sparse data and complex non-linear shifts associated with rare meteorological events. These methods typically treat environmental contexts as shallow numerical features, failing to capture the underlying semantic reasoning-why and how specific adverse conditions alter mobility behaviors across regions with different functional compositions. To address this limitation, we propose Multi-STG, a unified framework that integrates Large Language Model (LLM)-derived semantic priors with graph-based spatiotemporal learning. Multi-STG introduces an LLM-empowered semantic enhancement module that transforms comprehensive context descriptions such as weather, POIs, and holidays into high-level semantic embeddings, effectively providing a knowledge-driven complement to data scarcity. Furthermore, we design a multimodal attentional fusion mechanism to dynamically weigh heterogeneous inputs and a semantic-enhanced prediction head with gated residual calibration to robustly adjust forecasts based on semantic guidance. Extensive experiments on real-world multi-city private car datasets demonstrate that Multi-STG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, reducing MAE by 11.82\% on average relative to the strongest baseline across three cities, while maintaining stable performance under sparse and non-stationary conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Interpretability tools are usually built for one model at a time: a refusal direction, a truthfulness probe, or a steering vector is found with labeled data inside the model it explains. We ask whether such tools can be carried to other models through a map between residual streams fit only on unlabeled prompts. We transfer a refusal direction, tested causally by addition and ablation, and two read-out tools, a correctness direction and a sentiment probe, across 110 pairs of models from five families and 1B to 72B parameters and between base and instruction-tuned models, with controls for specificity and five seeds. How much transfers depends on what the map was fit on. A map fit on ordinary questions recovers 54\% of the target's own refusal steering and 16\% to 77\% of its correctness gain; adding unlabeled prompts from the region where the tool operates raises these to 102\% and 88\% to 98\%, while control directions recover at most 1\%. We prove that a linear map behaves this way whenever two models correspond linearly on the data it covers, and a label-free coverage score derived from this result tracks recovery across more than 16,000 runs and explains earlier reports that maps between models fail across domains. Carried read-out tools outperform anything the target's unlabeled representations yield. Interpretability results, once found, can be reused across models at the cost of a few unlabeled prompts.

Abstract: We introduce Sage-Taiyi, a metacognition-inspired architecture that uses accumulated scientific understanding to guide what to investigate next and how to investigate it. Through explicit monitoring and control, the system revises its understanding of evidence, assumptions, and unresolved questions, then uses these assessments to adapt research targets and select reusable reasoning methods. On Taiyi-Bench, an AI co-scientist ideation benchmark comprising 265 tasks from 50 papers across ten disciplines, Sage-Taiyi ranks first on all eight metrics of historical rediscovery and open-ended ideation, achieving an Overall score of 54.0 compared with 47.9 for the strongest baseline, AI Scientist-v2. It also performs well on two external scientific ideation benchmarks. Five expert-guided collaborations further connect its proposals to experimental and computational validation across chemistry, materials science, and engineering. These results support metacognitive regulation as an approach to scientific ideation and its progression toward practical validation.

Abstract: Log-based anomaly detection is increasingly dominated by large pretrained models, from BERT-scale encoders to 8B-parameter language models, which require GPUs at inference time and are difficult to deploy on the monitoring nodes where logs are produced. We present LogCDM, a compact detector that scores each log session using three complementary signals: the next-event surprise of a small causal Transformer, the distance between a graph-attention embedding of the session's own transition graph and that of a normal reference graph, and a transition-drift statistic that we show is exactly a frequency-weighted chi-squared divergence between transition kernels. The divergence view yields an asymptotic null distribution, a length-standardized score, and a per-edge decomposition that explains each alarm. A sigmoid gate fuses the three signals; under a shared-covariance Gaussian model, this gate is the Bayes posterior, and its learned weights indicate which signal each dataset relies on. With 8.1-8.8M parameters and no GPU required at inference, LogCDM reaches F1 scores of 0.984 on HDFS, 0.870 on chronologically split BGL, and 0.850 on Thunderbird, within 0.013-0.12 of an 8.1B-parameter LLM detector evaluated under the same protocol and above all compact baselines on BGL. It scores a session in 5.5-10.5 ms on a CPU. Ablations show that the structural signals add 0.025-0.030 F1 on BGL and Thunderbird but almost nothing on HDFS, consistent with the learned fusion weights.

Abstract: AI systems must often maintain bounded memory of unbounded interaction histories. However, optimizing the memory representation, and its updates, for downstream task utility is difficult for standard reinforcement learning (RL) due to severe credit assignment bottlenecks induced by sparse, delayed rewards. We introduce MaxIM (Maximally Informative Incremental Memory), a framework that formulates incremental summarization as a capacity-constrained, information-theoretic reward-shaping problem. Grounded in value suboptimality bounds, we show that bounding sequential information loss motivates maximizing the predictive log-likelihood of future, task-relevant targets, a variational lower bound on predictive sufficiency. We introduce a recursive consistency condition to ensure summaries are grounded by maximizing mutual information (MI) with the observed history. We mitigate credit assignment issues with a parameterized asymmetric critic that computes step-wise variational MI bounds for dense reward shaping. Empirical evaluation on long-horizon scientific reading and multi-turn dialogue benchmarks shows that MaxIM outperforms RL, supervised fine-tuning, and prompt-focused baselines on factual grounding, comprehensive autorater metrics, and downstream task utility across strict token budgets and extended horizons, without the need for expensive human preference labels.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) can improve an agent's task capability while reinforcing behavior that violates contextual execution requirements. We study this reward-driven misalignment, including reward hacking, through verifiable reward--context conflicts, where compliant task completion remains feasible while some executable shortcuts earn more reward than every compliant strategy. Encoding these conflicts in task dynamics lets us generate diverse interactive environments at scale and derive compliance feedback from execution histories. In resource and reporting workflows, task optimization increases real productivity through unauthorized resource use or raises reported scores while actual work declines. Controlled changes in the training distribution show that minority conflict exposure can still lead to widespread shortcut acquisition, reaching 97.9\% certified profitable violations on an API task under a 25\% conflict mixture after 128 updates. The same contextual checks supply a simple binary penalty that supports joint capability and compliance gains and partially mitigates acquired violations. Learned safety adapts to expanded authorization and transfers across mechanisms into workflows with evolving evidence and approval requirements. On 32 unseen changing-evidence tasks, resource-trained safety increases safe completion from 53.1\% to 89.1\% relative to task-only training, while both policies achieve full real work. These results connect a verifiable account of reward-driven misalignment with scalable task construction and safety learning that remains useful under changing execution requirements.
